Term Orders for Optimistic Lambda-Superposition
Alexander Bentkamp ORCID:0000-0002-7158-3595, Jasmin Blanchette ORCID:0000-0002-8367-0936, Matthias Hetzenberger ORCID:0000-0002-2052-8772

Abstract
. We introduce λ \lambda KBO and λ \lambda LPO, two variants of the Knuth–Bendix order (KBO) and the lexicographic path order (LPO) designed for use with the λ \lambda -superposition calculus. We establish the desired properties via encodings into the familiar first-order KBO and LPO.



[a]
[a]
[b]

1.
Introduction

The
\lambda
-superposition calculus, by Bentkamp et al.
[
3
]
, is a highly competitive

[
12
]
approach for proving higher-order problems
automatically. It works by saturation, performing inferences between available
clauses until the empty clause
\bot
is derived. A clause consists of literals,
which are predicates (often equality
\approx
) applied to arguments.
Terms are equivalence classes modulo the
\alpha
-,
\beta
-, and

\eta
-conversions of the
\lambda
-calculus. Thus,
\mathsf{f}
,
\lambda x.\>\mathsf{f}\>x
, and
(\lambda y.\>y)\>\mathsf{f}
are all considered syntactically
equal.

To break symmetries in the search space,
\lambda
-superposition uses an order

\succ
on the terms. The stronger the order, the fewer clauses need to be
generated to saturate the clause set. Yet the
derived higher-order orders

used by the only implementation of
\lambda
-superposition

[
3
, Sect. 3]
are a crude encoding in terms of a
standard term order, whether the Knuth–Bendix order (KBO) or the lexicographic
path order (LPO)
[
13
]
. It is very weak in the presence of applied
variables; for example, it cannot orient the terms
y\>\mathsf{b}
and

y\>\mathsf{a}
, even with the precedence
\mathsf{b}>\mathsf{a}
.

In this work, we introduce two stronger orders, called
\lambda
KBO and

\lambda
LPO. As the names suggest,
\lambda
KBO and
\lambda
LPO are variants
of KBO and LPO, which are the most widely used orders with superposition
calculi. KBO compares terms by first comparing their syntactic weight, resorting
to a lexicographic comparison as a tiebreaker. LPO essentially performs a
lexicographic comparison while ensuring the subterm property (i.e., the property
that a term is larger than its proper subterms). We define three versions of

\lambda
KBO and
\lambda
LPO, of increasing expressiveness: for ground
(i.e., closed) terms, monomorphic nonground (i.e., open) terms, and polymorphic
nonground terms.

The ground orders (Sect.
3
) form the first level of a
development by stepwise refinement. The monomorphic orders
(Sect.
4
) add support for term variables; their
properties are justified in terms of the ground level. Similarly, the
polymorphic orders (Sect.
5
) add support for type
variables on top of the monomorphic level. For
\lambda
KBO, weights are
computed as polynomials over indeterminates whose values depend on the variables
occurring in the terms. These polynomials can be compared symbolically.

Both orders are specified as a strict relation
\succ
and a nonstrict
relation
\succsim
, along the lines of Sternagel and Thiemann

[
11
]
. The nonstrict orders make the comparison

y\>\mathsf{b}\succsim y\>\mathsf{a}
possible if
\mathsf{b}>\mathsf{a}
. In this
example, a strict comparison would fail because
y
could be instantiated by

\lambda x.\>\mathsf{c}
, which ignores the argument and makes both terms equal.

A requirement imposed by the
optimistic
\lambda
-superposition
calculus,
for which the two orders are specifically designed, is that
the order must ensure
u\succ u\>\mathsf{diff}\langle\tau,\upsilon\rangle(s,t)

for a dedicated Skolem symbol
\mathsf{diff}
,
for all ground terms
s,t,u
, and for all ground types
\tau
and
\upsilon
.
This allows optimistic
\lambda
-superposition to provide special support for the
functional extensionality axiom

z\>(\mathsf{diff}\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle(\lambda\>z\>0,\lambda\>y\>0))\not\approx y\>(\mathsf{diff}\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle(\lambda\>z\>0,\lambda\>y\>0))\mathrel{\lor}(\lambda\>z\>0)\approx(\lambda\>y\>0)

Notice that the two arguments of the Skolem symbol
\mathsf{diff}
are
specified in parentheses, as mandatory arguments or parameters.

2.
Preliminaries

We use the notation
\bar{x}_{n}
or
\bar{x}
for tuples or lists
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}

of length
\left|\bar{x}\right|=n\geq 0
. Applying a unary function
f
to
such a tuple applies it pointwise:
f(\bar{x}_{n})=(f(x_{1}),\dots,f(x_{n}))
.

We write
\mathbb{N}
for the set of natural numbers starting with 0
and
\mathbb{N}_{>0}
for
\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
.
We write
\mathbf{O}
for the set of ordinals below
\epsilon_{0}
and
\mathbf{O}_{>0}
for

\mathbf{O}\setminus\{0\}
.

2.1.
Terms

We will need both untyped first-order and typed higher-order terms:

–

Given an untyped first-order signature
\Sigma
, we write
\cal T(\Sigma,X)

for the set of arity-respecting terms built using symbols from
\Sigma
and
the variables
X
—the (
\Sigma
-)
terms
. A first-order term
t
is

ground
if it contains no
variables, or equivalently if
t\in\cal T(\Sigma,\emptyset)
.

–

For the higher order, the types and terms are those of polymorphic
higher-order logic, as defined in Bentkamp et al.
[
3
]
, but with a few specificities noted below.

A higher-order signature
(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\Sigma)
consists of a type signature

\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}}
and a term signature
\Sigma
, which depends on

\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}}
. With each type constructor
\kappa\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}}
is associated
an arity—the number of arguments it takes. The set of types

\cal{Ty}(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\allowbreak X_{\mathsf{ty}})
over
X_{\mathsf{ty}}
is built using variables from
X_{\mathsf{ty}}
and
type constructors applied to the expected number of arguments. The functional
type constructor
\to
is distinguished. We abbreviate
\tau_{1}\to\cdots\to\tau_{n}\to\upsilon
to
\bar{\tau}_{n}\to\upsilon
.

The first departure from Bentkamp et al. is that we find it convenient to
represent
\lambda
-terms using a locally nameless notation

[
6
]
based on De Bruijn indices
[
5
]
. This
notation is essentially isomorphic to a nominal notation, with

\alpha
-equivalence built in. For example,
\lambda x.\,\lambda y.\,x
will be
represented as
\lambda\,\lambda\>1
, where the De Bruijn index
1

is a “nameless dummy.” We allow leaking De Bruijn indices—indices that
point beyond all
\lambda
-binders—but these will be ignored by substitutions.
The operator
t{\uparrow}^{n}
shifts all leaking De Bruijn indices by
n
; if
omitted,
n=1
.

The second departure from Bentkamp et al. is that we will use the
\eta
-long

\beta
-normal form as representatives for
\beta\eta
-equivalence classes, or
“
terms
,” where they used the
\eta
-short
\beta
-normal form. The main
advantage of
\eta
-long is that it makes it possible to obtain the desired
maximality result for the functional extensionality axiom. Moreover, since with

\eta
-long terms of function type are always
\lambda
-abstractions, we will
find that this simplifies the arithmetic when defining the
\lambda
KBO. Given
a
\lambda
-term
t
, we will denote its
\eta
-long
\beta
-normal form as

{t}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}
.

The main disadvantage of the
\eta
-long
\beta
-normal form arises with
polymorphism: Instantiating a type variable with a functional type can result in
an
\eta
-expansion, dramatically changing the term’s shape. For example, if
z:\alpha
, then
z\{\alpha\mapsto(\kappa\to\kappa)\}=\lambda\>z\>0
.
This can be accounted for by the orders, but only at the cost of some weakening.

The third departure from Bentkamp et al. is that the symbols (also called
constants) may take parameters, passed in parentheses, in addition to their
regular curried arguments. These parameters do not count as subterms. This
mechanism is used for
\mathsf{diff}
. Parameters are supported by the optimistic

\lambda
-superposition calculus.

We write
t:\tau
to indicate that
t
has type
\tau
. With each symbol

\mathsf{f}\in\Sigma
is associated a typing
\Pi\bar{\alpha}_{m}.\;\bar{\tau}_{n}\Rightarrow\upsilon
, where
\bar{\alpha}_{m}
is a tuple of distinct variables
that contains all type variables from
\bar{\tau}_{n}
and
\upsilon
,
\bar{\tau}_{n}

is the tuple of parameter types, and
\upsilon
is the (possibly
functional) body type. Given
\mathsf{f}
, we let
\cal{tyarity}(\mathsf{f})=m
and

\cal{arity}(\mathsf{f})=n
. We specify a type instance by specifying a tuple

\bar{\alpha}_{m}\sigma
of types in angle brackets corresponding to the type
arguments:
\mathsf{f}\langle\bar{\alpha}_{m}\sigma\rangle:\bar{\tau}_{n}\sigma\Rightarrow\upsilon\sigma
. Parameters are passed in parentheses.

The set of
\lambda
-preterms is built from the following expressions:

–

a variable
x\langle\tau\rangle:\tau
for
x\in X
and a type
\tau
;

–

a symbol
\mathsf{f}\langle\bar{\upsilon}_{m}\rangle(\bar{u}_{n}):\tau

for a constant
\mathsf{f}\in\Sigma
with type declaration
\Pi\bar{\alpha}_{m}.\>\bar{\tau}_{n}\Rightarrow\tau
, types
\bar{\upsilon}_{m}
,
and
\lambda
-preterms
\bar{u}:\bar{\tau}_{n}
such that all De Bruijn indices in
\bar{u}
are bound;

–

a De Bruijn index
n\langle\tau\rangle:\tau
for a natural number
n\geq 0
and a type
\tau
, where
\tau

represents the type of the bound variable;

–

a
\lambda
-expression
\lambda\langle\tau\rangle\>t:\tau\to\upsilon

for a type
\tau
and a
\lambda
-preterm
t:\upsilon

such that all De Bruijn indices bound by the new
\lambda\langle\tau\rangle

have type
\tau
;

–

an application
s\>t:\upsilon
for

\lambda
-preterms
s:\tau\to\upsilon
and
t:\tau
.

The type arguments
\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle
carry enough information to enable
typing of any
\lambda
-preterm without any context. We often leave them
implicit, when they are irrelevant or can be inferred.
In
\mathsf{f}\langle\bar{\upsilon}_{m}\rangle(\bar{u}_{n}):\tau
,
we call
\bar{u}_{n}
the parameters.
We omit
()
when a symbol has no parameters.
As a syntactic convenience, symbols
corresponding to infix operators are applied infix.
Notice that it is possible for a term to contain multiple occurrences of the same free De Bruijn index
with different types. In contrast, the types of bound De Bruijn indices always match.

A
\lambda
-term is a
\lambda
-preterm without free De Bruijn indices.

The size
|\phantom{j}|
of a
\lambda
-preterm is defined recursively by the
following equations:

\displaystyle|x|

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle|\mathsf{f}(\bar{u})|

\displaystyle=1+\sum\nolimits_{i}|u_{i}|

\displaystyle|n|

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle|\lambda\>t|

\displaystyle=1+|t|

\displaystyle|s\>t|

\displaystyle=|s|+|t|

The set
\cal T^{\infty}_{\mathrm{pre}}(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\allowbreak\Sigma,\allowbreak X_{\mathsf{ty}},\allowbreak X)
of
preterms
consists of the

\beta\eta
-equivalence classes of
\lambda
-preterms.
The set
\cal T^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\allowbreak\Sigma,\allowbreak X_{\mathsf{ty}},\allowbreak X)
of “
terms
” consists of the

\beta\eta
-equivalence classes of
\lambda
-terms.
Preterms have the following
four mutually exclusive forms, where
t,\bar{t}
are terms:

–

a fully applied variable
x\langle\tau\rangle\>\bar{t}
;

–

a fully applied symbol
\mathsf{f}\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle(\bar{u})\>\bar{t}
;

–

a fully applied De Bruijn index
n\langle\tau\rangle\>\bar{t}
;

–

a
\lambda
-abstraction
\lambda\langle\tau\rangle\>t
.

“Fully applied” means that the preterm as a whole has nonfunctional type.
The above view is reminiscent of first-order terms: The variable and symbol
cases are essentially as for first-order terms, De Bruijn indices are regarded
as symbols, and even the
\lambda
-abstraction
\lambda\>t
can be thought of as
a unary function application
\lambda(t)
. This will be the key to adapting the
first-order KBO and LPO to higher-order preterms.

A type
\tau
is monomorphic
if
\tau\in\cal{Ty}(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\allowbreak\emptyset)
—i.e., if it contains no variables; otherwise,
it is polymorphic.
A preterm is monomorphic if all its type arguments are monomorphic; otherwise, it
is polymorphic. A preterm
t
is
ground
if it is closed and monomorphic, or
equivalently if
t\in\cal T^{\infty}_{\mathrm{pre}}(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\allowbreak\Sigma,\allowbreak\emptyset,\allowbreak\emptyset)
.

Substitutions are defined as mappings from a set of type variables to types and
from term variables to terms of the same type.
A
monomorphizing
type substitution maps all
type variables to ground types and leaves term variables unchanged. A

grounding
substitution maps all variables to ground types and terms.

We will say that a preterm is
steady
if
its type is neither of function type nor a type variable.

Unless otherwise specified, all preterms will be presented in

\eta
-long normal form.

2.2.
Orders

{defi}

Given a binary relation
\succ
, we write
\succeq
for its reflexive
closure.

{defi}

Given a binary relation
\succ
, we write
\mathrel{\succ\!\!\!\!\!\succ}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}
for its left-to-right
lexicographic extension, defined as follows on same-length tuples:

()\mathrel{\succ\!\!\!\!\!\succ}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}()
does not hold, and for
n\geq 1
,

(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\allowbreak\mathrel{\succ\!\!\!\!\!\succ}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
holds if and only if

y_{1}\succ x_{1}
or else
y_{1}=x_{1}
and
(y_{2},\dots,y_{n})\mathrel{\succ\!\!\!\!\!\succ}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})
.

{defi}

Given binary relations
\succ
and
\succsim
, we write
\mathrel{{\succsim}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\vrule height=0.86108pt,depth=2.58334pt,width=7.3194pt}\!\!\!\!\!{\succsim}}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}
for
their left-to-right strict lexicographic extension, defined as follows on
same-length tuples:
()\mathrel{{\succsim}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\vrule height=0.86108pt,depth=2.58334pt,width=7.3194pt}\!\!\!\!\!{\succsim}}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}()
does not hold, and for
n\geq 1
,

(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\mathrel{{\succsim}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\vrule height=0.86108pt,depth=2.58334pt,width=7.3194pt}\!\!\!\!\!{\succsim}}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
holds if and only if

y_{1}\succ x_{1}
or else
y_{1}\succsim x_{1}
and
(y_{2},\dots,y_{n})\mathrel{{\succsim}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\vrule height=0.86108pt,depth=2.58334pt,width=7.3194pt}\!\!\!\!\!{\succsim}}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})
.

{defi}

Given binary relations
\succ
and
\succsim
, we write
\mathrel{{\succapprox}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\vrule height=1.72218pt,depth=3.01385pt,width=7.3194pt}\!\!\!\!\!{\succapprox}}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}
for
their left-to-right nonstrict lexicographic extension, defined as follows on
same-length tuples:
()\mathrel{{\succapprox}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\vrule height=1.72218pt,depth=3.01385pt,width=7.3194pt}\!\!\!\!\!{\succapprox}}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}()
holds, and for
n\geq 1
,

(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\mathrel{{\succapprox}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\vrule height=1.72218pt,depth=3.01385pt,width=7.3194pt}\!\!\!\!\!{\succapprox}}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
holds if and only if

y_{1}\succ x_{1}
or else
y_{1}\succsim x_{1}
and
(y_{2},\dots,y_{n})\mathrel{{\succapprox}\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\vrule height=1.72218pt,depth=3.01385pt,width=7.3194pt}\!\!\!\!\!{\succapprox}}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})
.

{defi}

A
precedence

>
on a set
A
is a well-founded total order
>

on
A
.

The first-order Knuth–Bendix order will constitute a useful stepping stone.
Like the original
[
7
]
, the version we use is untyped.
Unlike the original, but like the transfinite KBO
[
10
]
,
it uses ordinal weights instead of natural numbers and supports argument
coefficients.

{defi}

Let
\cal w:\Sigma\to\mathbf{O}_{>0}
and

\cal k:\Sigma\times\mathbb{N}_{>0}\to\mathbf{O}_{>0}
.
Define the weight function
\cal W:\cal T(\Sigma,\emptyset)\to\mathbf{O}
recursively by

\displaystyle\cal W(x)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\cal W(\mathsf{f}(\bar{s}_{m}))

\displaystyle=\cal w(\mathsf{f})+\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{m}\cal k(\mathsf{f},i)\cal W(s_{i})

{defi}

Let
\cal w,\cal k,\cal W
be as in Definition
2.2
.
Let
>
be an order (typically, a precedence) on an untyped signature
\Sigma
.
The
strict first-order KBO

\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}
induced by
\cal w,\cal k,>
on
nonground
\Sigma
-terms is defined inductively so that
t\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}s
if every
variable occurring in
s
occurs at least as many times in
t
as in
s
and if
any of these conditions is met:

(1)

\cal W(t)>\cal W(s)
;

(2)

\cal W(t)=\cal W(s)
,

t=\mathsf{g}(\bar{t})
,
s=\mathsf{f}(\bar{s})
,
and
\mathsf{g}>\mathsf{f}
;

(3)

\cal W(t)=\cal W(s)
,

t=\mathsf{g}(\bar{t})
,
s=\mathsf{g}(\bar{s})
,
and
\bar{t}\mathrel{\succ\!\!\!\!\!\succ}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{kbo}}\bar{s}
.

{defi}

Let
>
be an order (typically, a precedence) on an untyped signature
\Sigma
.
The
strict first-order LPO

\succ_{\mathsf{lpo}}
induced by
>
on
nonground
\Sigma
-terms is defined inductively so that
t\succ_{\mathsf{lpo}}s
if any of
these conditions is met, where
t=\mathsf{g}(\bar{t}_{k})
:

(1)

t_{i}\succeq_{\mathsf{lpo}}s
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
;

(2)

s=\mathsf{f}(\bar{s})
,
\mathsf{g}>\mathsf{f}
, and

\cal{chkargs}(t,\bar{s})
;

(3)

s=\mathsf{g}(\bar{s})
,
\bar{t}\mathrel{\succ\!\!\!\!\!\succ}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{lpo}}\bar{s}
, and

\cal{chkargs}(t,\bar{s})

where
\cal{chkargs}(t,\bar{s}_{k})
if and only if
t\succ_{\mathsf{lpo}}s_{i}
for every
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
.

3.
The Ground Level

We start by defining the
\lambda
KBO and
\lambda
LPO on ground preterms. We
connect them to the first-order KBO and LPO via an encoding so that we can lift
various properties, such as totality, well-foundedness, and compatibility with a
wide class of contexts. For
\lambda
KBO, in addition to
\cal w
, we will use
the parameter
\cal w_{\lambda}
for the weight of a
\lambda
and
\cal w_{\mathsf{db}}

for the weight of a De Bruijn index.

For the rest of this paper, we fix a higher-order signature
(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\Sigma)
and two infinite sets of variables
X_{\mathsf{ty}},X
.

3.1.
\bm{\lambda}
KBO

{defi}

Let
\cal w:\Sigma\to\mathbf{O}_{>0}
,
\cal w_{\lambda},\cal w_{\mathsf{db}}\in\mathbf{O}_{>0}
,
and
\cal k:\Sigma\times\mathbb{N}_{>0}\to\mathbf{O}_{>0}
.
Define the ground weight function
\cal W_{\mathsf{g}}:\cal T^{\infty}_{\mathrm{pre}}(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\allowbreak\Sigma,\allowbreak\emptyset,\allowbreak\emptyset)\to\mathbf{O}_{>0}

recursively by

\displaystyle\cal W_{\mathsf{g}}(\mathsf{f}(\bar{u})\>\bar{t}_{n})

\displaystyle=\cal w(\mathsf{f})+\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}\cal k(\mathsf{f},i)\cal W_{\mathsf{g}}(t_{i})

\displaystyle\cal W_{\mathsf{g}}(m\>\bar{t}_{n})

\displaystyle=\cal w_{\mathsf{db}}+\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}\cal W_{\mathsf{g}}(t_{i})

\displaystyle\cal W_{\mathsf{g}}(\lambda\>t)

\displaystyle=\cal w_{\lambda}+\cal W_{\mathsf{g}}(t)

{defi}

Let
\cal w_{\mathsf{ty}}:\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}}\to\mathbf{O}_{>0}
.
Let
>^{\mathsf{ty}}
be a precedence on
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}}
.
Let
\succ_{\mathsf{ty}}
be the strict first-order KBO induced by
\cal w_{\mathsf{ty}}
and

>^{\mathsf{ty}}
on
\cal T(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\emptyset)
.
Let
\cal w,\cal w_{\lambda},\cal w_{\mathsf{db}},\cal k,\cal W_{\mathsf{g}}
be as in
Definition
3.1
.
Let
>
be a precedence on
\Sigma
.

The
strict ground
\lambda
KBO

\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
induced by
\cal w_{\mathsf{ty}},\cal w,\allowbreak\cal w_{\lambda},\allowbreak\cal w_{\mathsf{db}},\allowbreak\cal k,\allowbreak>^{\mathsf{ty}},>
on
\cal T^{\infty}_{\mathrm{pre}}(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\allowbreak\Sigma,\allowbreak\emptyset,\allowbreak\emptyset)
is defined inductively
so that
t\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
if any of these conditions is met:

(1)

\cal W_{\mathsf{g}}(t)>\cal W_{\mathsf{g}}(s)
;

(2)

\cal W_{\mathsf{g}}(t)=\cal W_{\mathsf{g}}(s)
,

t
is of the form
\lambda\langle\upsilon\rangle\>t^{\prime}
, and
any of these conditions is met:

(a)

s
is of the form
\lambda\langle\tau\rangle\>s^{\prime}
and

\upsilon\succ_{\mathsf{ty}}\tau
, or

(b)

s
is of the form
\lambda\langle\upsilon\rangle\>s^{\prime}

and
t^{\prime}\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s^{\prime}
, or

(c)

s
is of the form
m\>\bar{s}
or

\mathsf{f}(\bar{u})\>\bar{s}
;

(3)

\cal W_{\mathsf{g}}(t)=\cal W_{\mathsf{g}}(s)
,
t
is of the form

n\>\bar{t}
, and
any of these conditions is met:

(a)

s
is of the form
m\>\bar{s}
and
n>m
, or

(b)

s
is of the form
n\>\bar{s}
and

\bar{t}\mathrel{\succ\!\!\!\!\!\succ}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}\bar{s}
, or

(c)

s
is of the form
\mathsf{f}(\bar{u})\>\bar{s}
;

(4)

\cal W_{\mathsf{g}}(t)=\cal W_{\mathsf{g}}(s)
,
t
is of the form

\mathsf{g}\langle\bar{\upsilon}\rangle(\bar{w})\>\bar{t}
, and
any of these conditions is met:

(a)

s
is of the form
\mathsf{f}(\bar{u})\>\bar{s}
and

\mathsf{g}>\mathsf{f}
, or

(b)

s
is of the form
\mathsf{g}\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle(\bar{u})\>\bar{s}
and

\bar{\upsilon}\mathrel{\succ\!\!\!\!\!\succ}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{ty}}\bar{\tau}
, or

(c)

s
is of the form
\mathsf{g}\langle\bar{\upsilon}\rangle(\bar{u})\>\bar{s}
and

(\bar{w},\bar{t})\mathrel{\succ\!\!\!\!\!\succ}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}(\bar{u},\bar{s})
.

In rule
3b
, we assume that leaking De Bruijn indices in

t
and
s
refer to the same variable and hence have the same type. This
invariant is preserved by the recursive application in
rule
2b
. A more defensive approach would be to compare the
types first and then the argument tuples as a tie breaker, as in rules

4b
and
4c
.

{defi}

Given a higher-order signature
(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\Sigma)
, let

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\mathsf{fo}}\kern 0.83328pt=\kern 0.83328pt

\displaystyle\{\mathsf{f}_{\bar{u}}^{\bar{\tau}}\mid\mathsf{f}\in\Sigma{,}\;\bar{\tau}\in(\cal{Ty}(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\allowbreak\emptyset))^{\cal{tyarity}(\mathsf{f})}{,}\;\bar{u}\in(\cal T^{\infty}_{\mathrm{pre}}(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\allowbreak\Sigma,\allowbreak\emptyset,\allowbreak\emptyset))^{\cal{arity}(\mathsf{f})}\}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\uplus}\{\mathsf{db}_{k}^{i}\mid i{,}\;k\in\mathbb{N}\}\mathrel{\uplus}\{\mathsf{lam}^{\tau}\mid\tau\in\cal{Ty}(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\allowbreak\emptyset)\}

be an untyped first-order signature.

{defi}

The translation
\cal{E}
defined by the following equations encodes

\cal T^{\infty}_{\mathrm{pre}}(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\allowbreak\Sigma,\allowbreak\emptyset,\allowbreak\emptyset)
into
\cal T(\Sigma_{\mathsf{fo}},\emptyset)
:

\displaystyle\cal{E}(\mathsf{f}\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle(\bar{u})\>\bar{t})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{f}_{\bar{u}}^{\bar{\tau}}(\cal{E}(\bar{t}))

\displaystyle\cal{E}(m\>\bar{t}_{n})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{db}_{n}^{m}(\cal{E}(\bar{t}_{n}))

\displaystyle\cal{E}(\lambda\langle\tau\rangle\>t)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{lam}^{\tau}}(\cal{E}(t))

Lemma 1
.

The translation
\cal{E}
is injective on ground terms.

Proof 3.1
.

By straightforward induction on
\cal{E}
’s definition.
Since we claim injectivity only for terms, not all preterms,
there is no need the type of a De Bruijn index.
The type of a De Bruijn
index is given by the corresponding enclosing
\mathsf{lam}^{\tau}
.
Similarly, the type of a parameter is
given by the function it is passed to.

It will be useful to apply
\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}
to encoded terms. Let the symbol weights

\cal w_{\mathsf{fo}}
and coefficients
\cal k_{\,\,\mathsf{fo}}
be derived from
\cal w
as follows:

\displaystyle\cal w_{\mathsf{fo}}(\mathsf{f}_{\bar{u}}^{\bar{\tau}})

\displaystyle=\cal w(\mathsf{f})

\displaystyle\cal w_{\mathsf{fo}}(\mathsf{db}^{i}_{k})

\displaystyle=\cal w_{\mathsf{db}}

\displaystyle\cal w_{\mathsf{fo}}(\mathsf{lam}^{\tau})

\displaystyle=\cal w_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\cal k_{\,\,\mathsf{fo}}(\mathsf{f}_{\bar{u}}^{\bar{\tau}},i)

\displaystyle=\cal k(\mathsf{f},i)

\displaystyle\cal k_{\,\,\mathsf{fo}}(\mathsf{db}^{j}_{k},i)

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\cal k_{\,\,\mathsf{fo}}(\mathsf{lam}^{\tau},i)

\displaystyle=1

Next, let
>^{\mathsf{kbo}}
be the
precedence on
\Sigma_{\mathsf{fo}}
that sorts the elements as follows,
from smallest to largest:

(1)

Start with the symbols
\mathsf{f}_{\bar{u}}^{\bar{\tau}}
in

<
-increasing order of their symbols
\mathsf{f}
, using
\mathrel{\prec\!\!\!\!\!\prec}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{ty}}
on
their superscripts as first tiebreaker and
\mathrel{\prec\!\!\!\!\!\prec}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
on the subscripts
as second tiebreaker.

(2)

Continue with the De Bruijn indices:

\mathsf{\mathsf{db}^{0}_{0}},\mathsf{\mathsf{db}^{0}_{1}},\dots
,
followed by
\mathsf{\mathsf{db}^{1}_{0}},\mathsf{\mathsf{db}^{1}_{1}},\dots
,

\mathsf{\mathsf{db}^{2}_{0}},\allowbreak\mathsf{\mathsf{db}^{2}_{1}},\dots,
and so on.

(3)

Conclude with the symbols
\mathsf{lam}^{\tau}
in
\prec_{\mathsf{ty}}
-increasing order
of their superscripts.

This definition ensures that symbols from
\Sigma
are smallest and

\mathsf{lam}^{\tau}
symbols are largest.

Let
\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}
denote the first-order KBO instance induced by
\cal w_{\mathsf{fo}},\cal k_{\,\,\mathsf{fo}},>^{\mathsf{kbo}}
, and let
\cal W_{\mathsf{kbo}}
denote its weight function. The
translation
\cal{E}
is faithful in the following sense:

Lemma 2
.

\cal W_{\mathsf{kbo}}(\cal{E}(u))=\cal W_{\mathsf{g}}(u)
for every
u\in\cal T^{\infty}_{\mathrm{pre}}(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\allowbreak\Sigma,\allowbreak\emptyset,\allowbreak\emptyset)
.

Proof 3.2
.

By induction on the definition of
\cal W_{\mathsf{g}}
.

Lemma 3
.

Given
s,t\in\cal T^{\infty}_{\mathrm{pre}}(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\allowbreak\Sigma,\allowbreak\emptyset,\allowbreak\emptyset)
, we have

t\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
if and only if
\cal{E}(t)\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}\cal{E}(s)
.

Proof 3.3
.

By Lemma
2
, any preterm
u
has the same weight according to

\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
as
\cal{E}(u)
according to
\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}
. The rules for establishing
t\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
and
\cal{E}(t)\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}\cal{E}(s)
correspond according to the following
table:

\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}

\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}

Rule
1

Rule
1

Rule
2

Rule
2a
,

2c
,

3a
,

3c
,

4a
,

4b
, or

4c

Rule
3

Rule

2b
,

3b
, or

4c

The equivalence can then be established by two proofs by induction on the
definition on
\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}
and
\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
, one for each direction of the
equivalence.

Theorem 4
.

The relation
\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
is a strict partial order.

Proof 3.4
.

This amounts to proving irreflexivity, antisymmetry, and transitivity.
The strategy is always the same and is illustrated for irreflexivity below.

Irreflexivity
: We must show
t\not\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}t
. By Lemma
3
, this
amounts to showing
\cal{E}(t)\not\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}\cal{E}(t)
, which is obvious since

\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}
is irreflexive.

Lemma 5
.

The relation
>^{\mathsf{kbo}}
is a precedence.

Proof 3.5
.

It is easy to see that the relation is total. For well-foundedness, suppose
there exists an infinite descending chain
\mathsf{g}_{0}>^{\mathsf{kbo}}\mathsf{g}_{1}>^{\mathsf{kbo}}\cdots
.

We say that a symbol
\mathsf{g}
is
bad
if there exists an infinite
chain
\mathsf{g}>^{\mathsf{kbo}}\cdots
.
Let us define the size
\|\phantom{j}\|
of a symbol as follows:

\displaystyle\|\mathsf{f}_{\bar{u}}^{\bar{\sigma}}\|

\displaystyle=1+\sum\nolimits_{i}\|u_{i}\|

\displaystyle\|\mathsf{db}^{i}_{k}\|

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\|\mathsf{lam}^{\tau}\|

\displaystyle=1

We can assume without loss of generality that the chain

\mathsf{g}_{0}>^{\mathsf{kbo}}\mathsf{g}_{1}>^{\mathsf{kbo}}\cdots
is minimal in the following sense:
\mathsf{g}_{0}
has
minimal size among bad symbols, and each
\mathsf{g}_{i+1}
has minimal size
among bad symbols
\mathsf{g}
such that
\mathsf{g}_{i}>^{\mathsf{kbo}}\mathsf{g}
.

The chain must have infinitely many steps of type 1, 2,
or 3. Since all steps of the same type are grouped together, there must exist an
index
k
from which all steps are of the same type. We distinguish three cases,
corresponding to the three types.

Case 1:
The chain
\mathsf{g}_{k}>^{\mathsf{kbo}}\mathsf{g}_{k+1}>^{\mathsf{kbo}}\cdots
, where
each symbol
\mathsf{g}_{i}
is of the form
\mathsf{f}_{\bar{u}}^{\bar{\tau}}
, is also an
infinite descending chain with respect to the lexicographic order induced by

<_{\mathsf{g}}
,
\mathrel{\prec\!\!\!\!\!\prec}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{ty}}
(for a fixed length
n
given by
\cal{tyarity}
), and

\mathrel{\prec\!\!\!\!\!\prec}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
(for a fixed length given by
\cal{arity}
). Both
<_{\mathsf{g}}
and

\mathrel{\prec\!\!\!\!\!\prec}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{ty}}
are well founded, so there must exist an index
l
from which
the symbol
\mathsf{f}
and its superscript
\bar{\tau}
are fixed, and only the
subscripts
\bar{u}
change. This means that we have an infinite chain of the
form
(\bar{u}_{n})_{l}\mathrel{\succ\!\!\!\!\!\succ}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}(\bar{u}_{n})_{l+1}\mathrel{\succ\!\!\!\!\!\succ}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}\cdots
.
By Lemma
3
, there would also exist a chain

\cal{E}((\bar{u}_{n})_{l})\mathrel{\succ\!\!\!\!\!\succ}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{kbo}}\cal{E}((\bar{u}_{n})_{l+1})\mathrel{\succ\!\!\!\!\!\succ}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{kbo}}\cdots
.

Since the bounded lexicographic order is well founded, this means that there
exists an infinite chain of the form
\cal{E}(v_{l})\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}\cal{E}(v_{l+1})\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}\cdots
.
Recall that the standard KBO is well founded if the underlying precedence is
well founded. If it is not, the standard well-foundedness argument tells us that
there must exist an infinite chain of distinct head symbols

\mathsf{h}_{0}>^{\mathsf{kbo}}\mathsf{h}_{1}>^{\mathsf{kbo}}\cdots
. Clearly,

\mathsf{h}_{0}
is both bad and smaller than
\mathsf{g}_{0}
, contradicting the
minimality of
\mathsf{g}_{0}
.

Case 2:
The chain
\mathsf{f}_{k}>^{\mathsf{kbo}}\mathsf{f}_{k+1}>^{\mathsf{kbo}}\cdots

corresponds to an infinite descending chain with respect to the lexicographic
order on pairs of natural numbers. Since that order is well founded, the chain
is impossible.

Case 3:
From
\mathsf{lam}^{\tau}_{0}>^{\mathsf{kbo}}\mathsf{lam}^{\tau}_{1}>^{\mathsf{kbo}}\cdots
, we extract
an infinite chain
\tau_{0}\succ_{\mathsf{ty}}\tau_{1}\succ_{\mathsf{ty}}\cdots
, contradicting the
well-foundedness of the first-order KBO.

Theorem 6
.

The relation
\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
is total on ground terms.

Proof 3.6
.

Assume
t\not=s
. We must show that
t\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
or
t\prec_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
.
Note that by Lemma
1
,
\cal{E}(t)\not=\cal{E}(s)
.
Hence, by totality of
\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}
, either
\cal{E}(t)\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}\cal{E}(s)
or

\cal{E}(t)\prec_{\mathsf{kbo}}\cal{E}(s)
. We obtain the desired result by applying
Lemma
3
twice.

Theorem 7
.

The relation
\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
is well founded.

Proof 3.7
.

This follows again straightforwardly by Lemma
3
.
If there existed an infinite chain
t_{0}\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}t_{1}\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}\cdots
, there
would also exist an infinite chain
\cal{E}(t_{0})\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}\cal{E}(t_{1})\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}\cdots
,
contradicting the well-foundedness of
\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}
.

The
\lambda
-superposition calculus relies on notions of green and orange
subterms: the core inference rules use green subterms, whereas optional
simplification rules use orange subterms. Since all green subterms are orange
subterms, we focus on the latter.

{defi}

Orange subterms
are defined inductively on ground preterms as follows:

(1)

Every preterm is an orange subterm of itself.

(2)

Every orange subterm of an argument
s_{i}
in
\mathsf{f}(\bar{t})\>\bar{s}
is
an orange subterm of
\mathsf{f}(\bar{t})\>\bar{s}
.

(3)

Every orange subterm of an argument
s_{i}
in
m\>\bar{s}
is an
orange subterm of
m\>\bar{s}
.

(4)

Every orange subterm of
u
is an orange subterm of
\lambda\>u
.

The context
u[\phantom{i}]
surrounding an orange subterm
s
of
u[s]
is
called an
orange context
. The notation
u\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,s\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}
indicates
that
s
is an orange subterm in
u[s]
, and
u\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,\phantom{i}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}

indicates that
u[\phantom{i}]
is an orange context. The
depth
of an
orange context is the number of
\lambda
s in
u[\phantom{i}]
that have the
hole in their scope.

{defi}

A relation
\succ
is
compatible with orange contexts
if
t\mathbin{\succ}s
implies

u\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,t{\uparrow}^{k}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\allowbreak\succ u\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,s{\uparrow}^{k}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}

for every orange context
u\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,\phantom{i}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}
, where
k
is its
depth.
The relation
\succ
enjoys the
orange subterm property
if

u\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,s{\uparrow}^{k}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\succeq s

for every orange context
u\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,\phantom{i}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}
, where
k
is its
depth.

Theorem 8
.

The relation
\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
is compatible with orange contexts.

Proof 3.8
.

Let
u\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,\phantom{i}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}
be an orange context of depth
k
.
Assume
t\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
. Note that by Lemma
3
,
\cal{E}(t)\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}\cal{E}(s)
.
Moreover, by inspection of the rules of
\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}
, we find that

\cal{E}(t{\uparrow}^{k})\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}\cal{E}(s{\uparrow}^{k})
.
This works because we give all De Bruijn indices the same weight, and the
precedence of indices remains stable under shifting.

Now, observe that orange subterms are mapped to first-order subterms by

\cal{E}
. In particular, there exists a first-order context
v[\phantom{i}]

such that
\cal{E}(u\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,t{\uparrow}^{k}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture})=v[\cal{E}(t{\uparrow}^{k})]
and

\cal{E}(u\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,s{\uparrow}^{k}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture})=v[\cal{E}(s{\uparrow}^{k})]
. By
compatibility of
\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}
with contexts, we have
v[\cal{E}(t{\uparrow}^{k})]\allowbreak\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}v[\cal{E}(s{\uparrow}^{k})]
. Thus, by
Lemma
3
, we get
u\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,t{\uparrow}^{k}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}u\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,s{\uparrow}^{k}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}
, as desired.

Theorem 9
.

The relation
\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
has the orange subterm property.

Proof 3.9
.

The key idea is as in the proof of Theorem
8
.
For any orange context
u\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,\phantom{i}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}
of depth
k
,
there exists a first-order context
v[\phantom{i}]
such that

\cal{E}(u\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,s{\uparrow}^{k}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture})=v[\cal{E}(s{\uparrow}^{k})]
.
By the subterm property of
\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}
, we have

v[\cal{E}(s{\uparrow}^{k})]\geq_{\mathsf{kbo}}\cal{E}(s{\uparrow}^{k})
.
By inspection of the rules of
\succ_{\mathsf{kbo}}
, we also have

\cal{E}(s{\uparrow}^{k})\geq_{\mathsf{kbo}}\cal{E}(s)
.
By transitivity and Lemma
3
,
we get
u\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{45.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(1.1,2.5){\rotatebox{315.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\,s{\uparrow}^{k}\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\!\!\begin{picture}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{135.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\put(0.0,2.5){\rotatebox{225.0}{\line{1}{0}{6.0}}}\end{picture}\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
, as desired.

The last property is necessary for
\lambda
-superposition. It is easy to prove.

Proof 3.10
.

This follows straightforwardly from the definition of
\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
.

The

-superposition calculus also specifies a requirement on applied

Theorem 11
.

Assume
\cal w(\mathsf{diff})\leq\cal w_{\mathsf{db}}
and
\cal k(\mathsf{diff},i)=1
for every
i
.
For all ground types
\tau,\upsilon
and ground preterms
s,t,u:\tau\to\upsilon
,
we have
u\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}u\>\mathsf{diff}\langle\tau,\upsilon\rangle(s,t)
.

Proof 3.11
.

Since
u
is of type
\tau\to\upsilon
, in its
\eta
-long normal form, it has the form
\lambda\>u^{\prime}
for some
u^{\prime}
.
Since
\mathsf{diff}\langle\tau,\upsilon\rangle(s,t)
is a symbol, we can obtain
the
\eta
-long
\beta
-normal form of
u\>\mathsf{diff}\langle\tau,\upsilon\rangle(s,t)

by replacing free De Bruijn indices of
u^{\prime}
by

\mathsf{diff}\langle\tau,\upsilon\rangle(s,t)
.
Since
\cal w(\mathsf{diff})\leq\cal w_{\mathsf{db}}
and
\cal k(\mathsf{diff},i)=1
for every
i
,
it follows that

\cal W_{\mathsf{g}}(u\>\mathsf{diff}\langle\tau,\upsilon\rangle(s,t))\leq\cal W_{\mathsf{g}}(u^{\prime})<W_{\mathsf{g}}(u)
.
Therefore,

u\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}u\>\mathsf{diff}\langle\tau,\upsilon\rangle(s,t)

by rule
1
.

3.2.
\bm{\lambda}
LPO

{defi}

Let
>^{\mathsf{ty}}
be a precedence on
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}}
.
Let
\succ_{\mathsf{ty}}
be the strict first-order LPO induced by
>^{\mathsf{ty}}
on

\cal T(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\emptyset)
.
Let
>
be a precedence on
\Sigma
.
Let
\mathsf{ws}\in\Sigma
be a distinguished element called the

watershed
.

The
strict ground
\lambda
LPO

\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
induced by
>^{\mathsf{ty}},>

on
\cal T^{\infty}_{\mathrm{pre}}(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\allowbreak\Sigma,\allowbreak\emptyset,\allowbreak\emptyset)
is defined inductively so that
t\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
if any of
these conditions is met:

(1)

t
is of the form
\mathsf{g}\langle\bar{\upsilon}\rangle(\bar{w})\>\bar{t}_{k}
and
any of these conditions is met:

(a)

t_{i}\succeq_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, or

(b)

s=\mathsf{f}(\bar{u})\>\bar{s}
,
\mathsf{g}>\mathsf{f}
, and

\cal{chkargs}(t,\bar{s})
, or

(c)

s=\mathsf{g}\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle(\bar{u})\>\bar{s}
,
\bar{\upsilon}\mathrel{\succ\!\!\!\!\!\succ}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{ty}}\bar{\tau}
,
and
\cal{chkargs}(t,\bar{s})
, or

(d)

s=\mathsf{g}\langle\bar{\upsilon}\rangle(\bar{u})\>\bar{s}
,

(\bar{w},\bar{t})\mathrel{\succ\!\!\!\!\!\succ}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}(\bar{u},\bar{s})
,
and
\cal{chkargs}(t,\bar{s})
, or

(e)

\mathsf{g}>\mathsf{ws}
,

s
is of the form
m\>\bar{s}
and
\cal{chkargs}(t,\bar{s})

or of the form
\lambda\>s^{\prime}
and
\cal{chkargs}(t,\allowbreak[s^{\prime}])
;

(2)

t
is of the form
n\>\bar{t}_{k}
and any of these
conditions is met:

(a)

t_{i}\succeq_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, or

(b)

s=m\>\bar{s}
,
n>m
, and
\cal{chkargs}(t,\bar{s})
, or

(c)

s=n\>\bar{s}
,
\bar{t}\mathrel{\succ\!\!\!\!\!\succ}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}\bar{s}
, and

\cal{chkargs}(t,\bar{s})
, or

(d)

s
is of the form
\lambda\>s^{\prime}
and
\cal{chkargs}(t,[s^{\prime}])
or
of the form or
\mathsf{f}(\bar{u})\>\bar{s}
,
where
\mathsf{f}\leq\mathsf{ws}
,
and
\cal{chkargs}(t,\bar{s})
;

(3)

t
is of the form
\lambda\langle\upsilon\rangle\>t^{\prime}
and any of these
conditions is met:

(a)

t^{\prime}\succeq_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
, or

(b)

s=\lambda\langle\tau\rangle\>s^{\prime}
,
\upsilon\succ_{\mathsf{ty}}\tau
, and

\cal{chkargs}(t,[s^{\prime}])
, or

(c)

s=\lambda\langle\upsilon\rangle\>s^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s^{\prime}
, or

(d)

s
is of the form
\mathsf{f}(\bar{u})\>\bar{s}
,
where
\mathsf{f}\leq\mathsf{ws}
,
and
\cal{chkargs}(t,\bar{s})

where
\cal{chkargs}(t,\bar{s}_{k})
if and only if
t\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s_{i}
for every
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
. The notation
[\phantom{i}]
is used to represent lists—here,
the singleton list.

Let
\Sigma_{\mathsf{fo}}
be a first-order signature as defined in
Sect.
3.1
. Let
>^{\mathsf{lpo}}
be the precedence on

\Sigma_{\mathsf{fo}}
that orders the elements as follows,
from smallest to largest:

(1)

Start with the symbols
\mathsf{f}_{\bar{u}}^{\bar{\tau}}
such that

\mathsf{f}\leq\mathsf{ws}
in

<
-increasing order of their symbols
\mathsf{f}
, using
\mathrel{\prec\!\!\!\!\!\prec}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{ty}}
on
their superscripts as first tiebreaker and
\mathrel{\prec\!\!\!\!\!\prec}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
on the subscripts
as second tiebreaker.

(2)

Continue with the symbols
\mathsf{lam}^{\tau}
in
\prec_{\mathsf{ty}}
-increasing order
of their superscripts.

(3)

Continue with the De Bruijn indices:

\mathsf{\mathsf{db}^{0}_{0}},\mathsf{\mathsf{db}^{0}_{1}},\dots
,
followed by
\mathsf{\mathsf{db}^{1}_{0}},\mathsf{\mathsf{db}^{1}_{1}},\dots
,

\mathsf{\mathsf{db}^{2}_{0}},\allowbreak\mathsf{\mathsf{db}^{2}_{1}},\dots,
and so on.

(4)

Conclude with the symbols
\mathsf{f}_{\bar{u}}^{\bar{\tau}}
such that

\mathsf{f}>\mathsf{ws}
in

<
-increasing order of their symbols
\mathsf{f}
, using
\mathrel{\prec\!\!\!\!\!\prec}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{ty}}
on
their superscripts as first tiebreaker and
\mathrel{\prec\!\!\!\!\!\prec}^{\smash{\mathsf{l}}\mathsf{ex}}_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
on the subscripts
as second tiebreaker.

This definition ensures that symbols below the watershed are smallest and
symbols above the watershed are largest. When considering polymorphism, we will
see that it is advantageous to put symbols above the watershed. However, the
special symbol
\mathsf{diff}
belongs below the watershed.

Lemma 12
.

The relation
>^{\mathsf{lpo}}
is a precedence

Proof 3.12
.

The proof is analogous to that of Lemma
5
.

Let
\succ_{\mathsf{lpo}}
denote the first-order LPO instance induced by the precedence

>^{\mathsf{lpo}}
.

Lemma 13
.

Given
s,t\in\cal T^{\infty}_{\mathrm{pre}}(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\allowbreak\Sigma,\allowbreak\emptyset,\allowbreak\emptyset)
, we have

t\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
if and only if
\cal{E}(t)\succ_{\mathsf{lpo}}\cal{E}(s)
.

Proof 3.13
.

The rules for establishing
t\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
and
\cal{E}(t)\succ_{\mathsf{lpo}}\cal{E}(s)
correspond
according to the following table:

\succ_{\mathsf{lpo}}

\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}

Rule
1

Rule
1a
,

2a
, or

3a

Rule
2

Rule

1b
,

1c
,

1d
,

1e
,

2b
,

2d
,

3b
, or

3d

Rule
3

Rule
1d
,

2c
, or

3c

The equivalence can then be established by two proofs by induction on the
definition on
\succ_{\mathsf{lpo}}
and
\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
, one for each direction of the
equivalence.
The only nontrivial case is that of rule
3c
of

\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
, because it lacks the
\cal{chkargs}
condition of the corresponding
rule
3
of
\succ_{\mathsf{lpo}}
.
Given
t\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
by rule
3c
,
to obtain
\cal{E}(t)\succ_{\mathsf{lpo}}\cal{E}(s)
by rule
3
,
we must show
\cal{chkargs}(\cal{E}(t),(\cal{E}(s^{\prime})))
.
We apply transitivity to combine
\cal{E}(t)\succ_{\mathsf{lpo}}\cal{E}(t^{\prime})
, which follows from the subterm property, and the induction
hypothesis
\cal{E}(t^{\prime})\succ_{\mathsf{lpo}}\cal{E}(s^{\prime})
.

Using Lemma
13
, we can prove the following theorems
using the same strategy as for Theorems

4
–
10
:

Theorem 14
.

The relation
\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
is a strict partial order.

Theorem 15
.

The relation
\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
is total on ground preterms.

Theorem 16
.

The relation
\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
is well founded.

Theorem 17
.

The relation
\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
is compatible with orange contexts.

Theorem 18
.

The relation
\succ_{\mathsf{g\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
has the orange subterm property.
