the same type.

Below we will connect the polymorphic
\lambda
KBO with its monomorphic
counterpart to lift its properties, which in turn were lifted from the ground

\lambda
KBO.

{defi}

The polynomial substitution
\cal{poly}(\theta)
associated with a monomorphizing
type substitution
\theta
maps indeterminate
\mathbf{w}_{y\>\bar{t}}
to

\mathbf{w}_{(y\>\bar{t})\theta}
, indeterminate
\mathbf{k}_{y\>\bar{t},i}
to

\mathbf{k}_{(y\>\bar{t})\theta,i}
, and indeterminate
\mathbf{h}_{\alpha}
to the
number of
\eta
-expansions incurred as a result of instantiating
\alpha
for
one preterm of type
\alpha
(excluding any subterms).

Lemma 38
.

Given a monomorphizing type substitution
\theta
, we have

\cal W(t){\big|}_{\cal{poly}(\theta)}=\cal W_{\mathsf{m}}(t\theta)
.

Proof 5.1
.

Let
\sigma=\cal{poly}(\theta)
. Let
t:\tau
, and let
k
be the number of

\eta
-expansions incurred as a result of applying
\theta
on a term of type

\tau
(excluding any subterms). The proof is by induction on the definition
of
\cal W
.

Case
t=y\>\bar{t}
:
We have

\displaystyle\cal W(y\>\bar{t}){\big|}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle{=}\;

\displaystyle 1+{\mathbf{w}_{y\>\bar{t}^{\>\star}}{\big|}_{\sigma}+\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{\smash{|\bar{t}^{\>\star\!\star}|}}\mathbf{k}_{y\>\bar{t}^{\>\star},i}{\big|}_{\sigma}(\cal W(\bar{t}^{\>\star\!\star}_{i}){\big|}_{\sigma}-\cal w_{\mathsf{db}})}+{\cal{H}(\tau){\big|}_{\sigma}}

by definition of
\cal W

\displaystyle{=}\;

\displaystyle 1+{\mathbf{w}_{y\>\bar{t}^{\>\star}}{\big|}_{\sigma}+\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{\smash{|\bar{t}^{\>\star\!\star}|}}\mathbf{k}_{y\>\bar{t}^{\>\star},i}{\big|}_{\sigma}(\cal W_{\mathsf{m}}(\bar{t}^{\>\star\!\star}_{i}\theta)-\cal w_{\mathsf{db}})}+{\cal{H}(\tau){\big|}_{\sigma}}

by the induction hypothesis

\displaystyle{=}\;

\displaystyle 1+{\mathbf{w}_{y\>\bar{t}^{\>\star}}{\big|}_{\sigma}+\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{\smash{|\bar{t}^{\>\star\!\star}|}}\mathbf{k}_{y\>\bar{t}^{\>\star},i}{\big|}_{\sigma}(\cal W_{\mathsf{m}}(\bar{t}^{\>\star\!\star}_{i}\theta)-\cal w_{\mathsf{db}})}+(\cal w_{\lambda}+\cal w_{\mathsf{db}})k

by definition of
\cal H
and the semantics of
\mathbf{h}

\displaystyle{=}\;

\displaystyle 1+\mathbf{w}_{(y\theta)\>(\bar{t}\theta)^{\>\star}}+\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{|(\bar{t}\theta)^{\>\star\!\star}|}\mathbf{k}_{(y\theta)\>(\bar{t}\theta)^{\>\star},i}(\cal W_{\mathsf{m}}(\bar{t}^{\>\star\!\star}_{i}\theta)-\cal w_{\mathsf{db}})+(\cal w_{\lambda}+\cal w_{\mathsf{db}})k

by definition of
\sigma

\displaystyle{=}\;

\displaystyle\cal W_{\mathsf{m}}((y\>\bar{t})\theta)

by definition of
\cal W_{\mathsf{m}}
and substitution

Case
t=\mathsf{f}(\bar{u})\>\bar{t}_{n}
:
We have

\displaystyle\cal W(\mathsf{f}(\bar{u})\>\bar{t}_{n}){\big|}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle{=}\;

\displaystyle\cal w(\mathsf{f})+\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}\cal k(\mathsf{f},i)\cal W(t_{i}){\big|}_{\sigma}+\cal H(\tau){\big|}_{\sigma}

by definition of
\cal W

\displaystyle{=}\;

\displaystyle\cal w(\mathsf{f})+\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}\cal k(\mathsf{f},i)\cal W_{\mathsf{m}}(t_{i}\theta)+\cal H(\tau){\big|}_{\sigma}

by the induction hypothesis

\displaystyle{=}\;

\displaystyle\cal w(\mathsf{f})+\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}\cal k(\mathsf{f},i)\cal W_{\mathsf{m}}(t_{i}\theta)+(\cal w_{\lambda}+\cal w_{\mathsf{db}})k

by definition of
\cal H

\displaystyle{=}\;

\displaystyle\cal W_{\mathsf{m}}((\mathsf{f}(\bar{u})\>\bar{t}_{n})\theta)

by definition of
\cal W_{\mathsf{m}}
, substitution, and

hypothesis
(\mathrm{K})

Case
t=m\>\bar{t}_{n}
:
Similar to the previous case.

Case
t=\lambda\>t
:
We have

\displaystyle\cal W(\lambda\>t){\big|}_{\sigma}

\displaystyle{=}\;

\displaystyle\cal w_{\lambda}+\cal W(t){\big|}_{\sigma}

by definition of
\cal W

\displaystyle{=}\;

\displaystyle\cal w_{\lambda}+\cal W_{\mathsf{m}}(t\theta)

by the induction hypothesis

\displaystyle{=}\;

\displaystyle\cal W_{\mathsf{m}}(\lambda\>(t\theta))

by definition of
\cal W_{\mathsf{m}}

\displaystyle{=}\;

\displaystyle\cal W_{\mathsf{m}}((\lambda\>t)\theta)

by definition of substitution

{defi}

Let
\sigma
be a substitution. Given a preterm
t
, let
t\sigma?
denote its

truncating substitution
, in which any outermost
\lambda
s introduced
due to
\eta
-expansion as a result of applying
\sigma
to
t
are omitted.
(In contrast, any introduced De Bruijn indices are kept.)

For example, if
\mathsf{c}:\alpha
and
\alpha\sigma=\kappa\to\kappa\to\kappa
, then
\mathsf{c}\sigma?=\mathsf{c}\>1\>0
, whereas

\mathsf{c}\sigma=\lambda\>\lambda\>\mathsf{c}\>1\>0
.

Theorem 39
.

If
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
, then
t\theta\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s\theta
for any monomorphizing
type substitution
\theta
.
If
t\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
, then
t\theta\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s\theta
for any
monomorphizing type substitution
\theta
.

Proof 5.2
.

The proof of the two claims is by induction on the shape of the derivation of
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s

and
t\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
.

For the first claim, we proceed by case distinction on the rule deriving
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
:

Rule
1
:
From
\cal W(t)>\cal W(s)
,
by Lemma
38
, we have

\cal W_{\mathsf{m}}(t\theta_{1})>\cal W_{\mathsf{m}}(s\theta_{1})
.
Thus, rule
1
of
\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
applies.

Rules
2
,
3
,
4
:
We have
\cal W(t)\geq\cal W(s)
. We also have
t\unrhd s
, either because
the rule requires it or because it follows from the types of
t
and
s
. We
perform a case analysis on
t\unrhd s
.

Subcase 1
, where
t
and
s
are of nonvariable types: The corresponding rule
2
,
3
, or

4
for
\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
applies. The only mismatch between the two
definitions is the use of
\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
versus
\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
, and it is repaired by the
induction hypothesis.

Subcase 2
, where
t
has some variable type
\alpha
but not

s
: If
\alpha\theta
is a function type, then applying
\theta
to
t

results in some
\eta
-expansion, which leads to a heavier weight;
rule
1
then applies. Otherwise,
\alpha\theta
is not a
function type, and the reasoning is as for subcase 1.

Subcase 3
, where
t
and
s
have some variable type
\alpha
: Let
k
be the number of curried arguments expected by values of type

\alpha\theta
. This means that we have

\displaystyle t\theta

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\lambda\ldots\lambda}_{k~\text{times}}\>(t\theta){\uparrow}^{k}\>{(k-1)}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}\>\ldots\>{0}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}

\displaystyle s\theta

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\lambda\ldots\lambda}_{k~\text{times}}\>(s\theta){\uparrow}^{k}\>{(k-1)}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}\>\ldots\>{0}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}

First, we apply rule
2b

k
times to remove the
leading
\lambda
s on both sides. It remains to show that
t\theta?\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s\theta?
. By inspection of the rules

3a
and
3b
of
\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
,
we find that
t{\uparrow}^{k}\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s{\uparrow}^{k}
. For each of the
\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
rules that
could have been used to establish this, we can check that the corresponding

\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
rule is applicable. The additional De Bruijn indices

{(k-1)}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}\>\ldots\>{0}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}
on both sides are harmless.

The proof of the second claim is analogous.

Theorem 40
.

The relation
\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
coincides with
\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
on monomorphic preterms.
The relation
\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
coincides with
\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
on monomorphic preterms.

Proof 5.3
.

One direction of the equivalences follows by
Theorem
39
. It remains to show that
t\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
implies
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
and that
t\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s

implies
t\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
. The proof is by induction on the definition of

\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
and
\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
. It is easy to see that to every case
in the definition of
\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
corresponds a case in the definition of

\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
and every case in the definition of
\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}

corresponds a case in the definition of
\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}
. For the weights,

\cal W_{\mathsf{m}}
and
\cal W
coincide. In particular, for a monomorphic preterm, the
polynomial returned by
\cal W
contains no
\mathbf{h}_{\alpha}
indeterminates.

Lemma 41
.

If
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
, then
t\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
.

Proof 5.4
.

Analogous to Lemma
23
.

Theorem 42
.

If
t\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}u
and
u\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
, then
t\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
.
If moreover
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}u
or
u\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
, then
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
.

Proof 5.5
.

Analogous to Theorem
29
,
using the fact that
\unrhd
is transitive.

Theorem 43
.

Let
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptkbo}}s
.
Let
\theta
be a substitution such that all variables in

t\theta
and
s\theta
are nonfunctional term variables.
Let
s\theta
contain a nonfunctional variable
x

outside of parameters.
Then
t\theta
must also contain
x
outside of parameters.

Proof 5.6
.

By Theorems
30
and
39
.

5.2.
\bm{\lambda}
LPO

{defi}

Let
>^{\mathsf{ty}}
be a precedence on
\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}}
.
Let
\succ_{\mathsf{ty}}
be the strict first-order LPO on
\cal T(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},X_{\mathsf{ty}})

induced by
>^{\mathsf{ty}}
.
Let
>
be a precedence on
\Sigma
.
Let
\mathsf{ws}\in\Sigma
be the watershed.

The
strict polymorphic
\lambda
LPO

\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
and the
nonstrict
polymorphic
\lambda
LPO

\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
induced by
>^{\mathsf{ty}},>
on

\cal T^{\infty}_{\mathrm{pre}}(\Sigma_{\mathsf{ty}},\allowbreak\Sigma,\allowbreak X_{\mathsf{ty}},\allowbreak X)
are defined by mutual induction. The strict
relation is defined so that
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
if

(1)

the rule

1a
,

1d
,

1e
,

2a
,

2c
,

3a
,

3b
, or

3c

of the definition of
\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
applies mutatis mutandi, or

(2)

the rule

1b
or

1c

of the definition of
\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
applies mutatis mutandi
and either
\mathsf{g}>\mathsf{ws}
or
t\unrhd s
holds, or

(3)

the rule

2b
,

2d
, or

3d

of the definition of
\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
applies mutatis mutandi
and
t\unrhd s
holds.

The nonstrict relation is defined so that
t\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
if

(1)

the rule

1
,

2a
,

2d
,

2e
,

3a
,

3c
,

4a
,

4b
, or

4c

of the definition of
\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
applies mutatis mutandi, or

(2)

the rule

2b
or

2c

of the definition of
\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
applies mutatis mutandi
and
\mathsf{g}>\mathsf{ws}
or
t\unrhd s
holds, or

(3)

the rule

3b
,

3d
, or

4d

of the definition of
\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
applies mutatis
mutandi and
t\unrhd s
holds.

Like for
\lambda
KBO, the conditions
t\unrhd s
are sometimes necessary to
guard against
\eta
-expansion on the right-hand side of
\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
.
However, they are not necessary in most cases. Consider the
precedence
\mathsf{h}>\mathsf{f}>\mathsf{a}
, with
\mathsf{h}>\mathsf{ws}
,
and suppose
\mathsf{a}:\alpha
,
\mathsf{h}:\kappa
.
We allow the polymorphic comparison
\mathsf{h}\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}\mathsf{a}

even though instantiating
\alpha
may lead to
\eta
-expansion of
\mathsf{a}
:

\mathsf{h}\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}\underbrace{\lambda\ldots\lambda}_{k~\text{times}}\>\mathsf{a}\>(k-1)\>\ldots\>0

The key for this to work is that symbols above the watershed—here,
\mathsf{h}
—are considered larger
than both
\lambda
s and De Bruijn indices.

Theorem 44
.

If
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
, then
t\theta\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}t\theta?\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s\theta
for any
monomorphizing type substitution
\theta
.
If
t\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
, then
t\theta\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s\theta
for any
monomorphizing type substitution
\theta
.

Proof 5.7
.

As an induction hypothesis, the inequality
t\theta?\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s\theta
will be
useful to apply rules that have a
\cal{chkargs}
condition.

First, we show
t\theta\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}t\theta?
. The only difference between the
two preterms is the presence of
k
additional
\lambda
s on the left. If
k=0
,
Lemma
31
can be used to establish
t\theta\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}t\theta?
. Otherwise, the property can be established by
applying rule
3a

k
times.

The proof of the two remaining inequalities is by induction on
|t|+|s|
.

Cases

1a
,
2a
,
3a
of

\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
:

These cases all correspond to “subterm” rules.
If
t
is of nonvariable type, we apply the corresponding rule of
\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
,
relying on the induction hypothesis for the recursive comparison with

\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
.
Otherwise, suppose
t
is of type
\alpha
. Let
k
be the number of
curried arguments expected by values of type
\alpha\theta
. This means that

t\theta?
is of the form

t^{\prime}{\uparrow}^{k}\>{(k-1)}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}\>\ldots\>{0}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}

We apply the rule of
\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
corresponding to the rule that was used to
establish
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
in the first place.

For this to work, a recursive comparison must be possible. We show how it can be
done for the case of rule
1a
of
\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
, where
t=\mathsf{f}(\bar{u})\>\bar{t}
; the other two cases are similar.
For rule
1a
to have been applicable to establish

\mathsf{f}(\bar{u})\>\bar{t}\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
, we must have
t_{i}\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
for some

i
.
Now, to apply rule
1a
to derive

\mathsf{f}(\bar{u}\theta{\uparrow}^{k})\>\bar{t}\theta{\uparrow}^{k}\>{(k-1)}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}\>\ldots\>{0}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s\theta

we must show that
t_{i}\theta{\uparrow}^{k}\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s\theta
.
From
t_{i}\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
, the induction hypothesis, and
by inspection of rules
3b
and
3c
of

\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
, we get
t_{i}\theta{\uparrow}^{k}\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}t_{i}\theta\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s\theta
, as desired.

Rules
1b
,
1c
,

1d
,
1e
of
\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
:
These cases have a symbol as the head on the left-hand side.
We will focus on the case of rule
1b
; the other three
cases are similar. For rule
1b
,

t=\mathsf{g}(\bar{w})\>\bar{t}
and
s=\mathsf{f}(\bar{u})\>\bar{s}
,
with
t\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s_{i}
for every
i
. We also have
either
\mathsf{g}>\mathsf{ws}
or
t\unrhd s
. We focus on the case
where
\mathsf{g}>\mathsf{ws}
; the other case is similar to that of
rule
2b
, below.

Let
t:\upsilon
and
s:\tau
. Let
l
and
k
be the number of curried
arguments expected by values of type
\upsilon\theta
and
\tau\theta
,
respectively. This means that we have

\displaystyle t\theta?

\displaystyle=\mathsf{g}(\bar{w}\theta{\uparrow}^{l})\>\bar{t}\theta{\uparrow}^{l}\>{(l-1)}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}\>\ldots\>{0}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}

\displaystyle s\theta

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\lambda\ldots\lambda}_{k~\text{times}}\>\mathsf{f}(\bar{u}\theta{\uparrow}^{k})\>\bar{s}\theta{\uparrow}^{k}\>{(k-1)}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}\>\ldots\>{0}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}

To show
t\theta?\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s\theta
, we apply rule
1e

k

times to remove the
\lambda
s on the right. It then suffices to prove

t\theta?\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s\theta?
. We apply rule
1b
. For the rule
to be applicable, due to the
\cal{chkargs}
condition
we need
t\theta?\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s_{i}\theta
to hold for every
i
. This
follows from
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s_{i}
and the induction hypothesis. In addition, we need

t\theta?\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}{j}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}
for
j\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}
. This follows from
rule
1e
.

Rules
2b
,
2c
of
\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
:

These cases compare applied De Bruijn indices
t=n\>\bar{t}
and

s=m\>\bar{s}
, where
n\geq m
. We also know that
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s_{i}
for
every
i
.
We perform a case analysis on
t\unrhd s
.

Subcase 1
, where
t
and
s
are of nonvariable types: Rule
2b
or
2c
of

\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
applies. For the rule to be applicable, due to the
\cal{chkargs}

condition we need
t\theta=t\theta?\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s_{i}\theta
to hold for every
i
.
This follows from
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s_{i}
and the induction hypothesis.

Subcase 2
, where
t
has some variable type
\alpha
but not

s
: If
\alpha\theta
is nonfunctional, the reasoning is as for subcase 1.
Otherwise,
\alpha\theta
is a function type, and applying
\theta
to
t

results in
k>0

\eta
-expansions. This means we have

\displaystyle t\theta?

\displaystyle=(n+k)\>(\bar{t}\theta){\uparrow}^{k}\>{(k-1)}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}\>\ldots\>{0}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}

\displaystyle s\theta

\displaystyle=m\>\bar{s}\theta

Since
n+k>m
, we apply rule
2b
to establish
t\theta?\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s\theta
. For the rule to be applicable,
due to the
\cal{chkargs}
condition

t\theta?\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s_{i}\theta
must hold for every
i
. This follows from

t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s_{i}
and the induction hypothesis.

Subcase 3
, where
t
and
s
has some variable type
\alpha
: Let
k
be the number of curried arguments expected by values of type

\alpha\theta
. This means that we have

\displaystyle t\theta?

\displaystyle=(n+k)\>(\bar{t}\theta){\uparrow}^{k}\>{(k-1)}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}\>\ldots\>{0}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}

\displaystyle s\theta

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\lambda\ldots\lambda}_{k~\text{times}}\>(m+k)\>(s\theta){\uparrow}^{k}\>{(k-1)}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}\>\ldots\>{0}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}

and must show
t\theta?\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s\theta
. First,
we apply rule
2d

k
times to remove the

\lambda
s on the right. For the rule to be applicable,
due to the
\cal{chkargs}
condition
t\theta?\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s\theta?
must hold.
To prove it, we apply rule
2b
or
2c
,
depending on whether
n>m
or
n=m
. For the tuple comparison in rule

2c
, it is easy to see that the additional De Bruijn
arguments are harmless. For either rule to be applicable,
due to the
\cal{chkargs}
condition we also need

t\theta?\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s_{i}\theta
for every
i
and
t\theta?\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}{j}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}

for every
j\in\{0,\ldots,k-1\}
.
The first inequality follows from
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s_{i}
and the induction hypothesis.
The second inequality follows from rule
2a
, since
one of the arguments in
t\theta?
is
{j}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}
.

Rule
2d
of
\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
:

This case compares an applied De Bruijn index
t=n\>\bar{t}
and either a

\lambda
-abstraction
\lambda\>s^{\prime}
or an applied symbol

\mathsf{f}(\bar{u})\>\bar{s}
below the watershed.
In the
\lambda
subcase, we have
t\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s^{\prime}
. We apply
rule
2d
to derive
t\theta?\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s\theta
. This
requires us to prove
t\theta?\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s^{\prime}\theta
, which follows from
t\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s^{\prime}
and the induction hypothesis.
In the other subcase, the proof is similar to as in cases

2b
,
2c
of
\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
above.

Rule
3b
of
\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
:

This case compares two
\lambda
-abstractions
t=\lambda\langle\upsilon\rangle\>t^{\prime}
and
s=\lambda\langle\tau\rangle\>s^{\prime}
.
We have
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s^{\prime}
. To derive the desired inequality

t\theta?=t\theta\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s\theta
,
we apply rule
3b
, which requires us to prove

\upsilon\theta\succ_{\mathsf{ty}}\tau\theta
and
t\theta\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s^{\prime}\theta
.
The first inequality follows from
\upsilon\succ_{\mathsf{ty}}\tau
by stability under
substitution of the standard LPO.
The second inequality follows from
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s^{\prime}
and the induction
hypothesis.

Rules
3c
of
\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
:

These cases compare two
\lambda
-abstractions
t=\lambda\langle\upsilon\rangle\>t^{\prime}
and
s=\lambda\langle\upsilon\rangle\>s^{\prime}
. We have
t^{\prime}\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s^{\prime}
. By the induction hypothesis,

t^{\prime}\theta\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s^{\prime}\theta
. By rule
3c
, we get

t\theta=\lambda\langle\upsilon\theta\rangle\>t^{\prime}\theta\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}\lambda\langle\upsilon\theta\rangle\>s^{\prime}\theta=s\theta
,
as desired.

Rule
3d
of
\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
:

This case compares a
\lambda
-abstraction

t=\lambda\langle\upsilon\rangle\>t^{\prime}
and an applied symbol

\mathsf{f}(\bar{u})\>\bar{s}
below the watershed.
The proof is similar to as in cases

2b
,
2c
of
\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
above.

Rule
1
of
\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
:

This case compares two preterms
y\>\bar{t}
and
y\>\bar{s}
headed by the same
variable and of the same type
\tau
.
Let
k
be the number of curried arguments expected by values of type

\tau\theta
. This means that we have

\displaystyle t\theta

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\vphantom{()}\lambda\ldots\lambda}_{k~\text{times}}\>\underbrace{\vphantom{()}\smash{(y\>\bar{t})\theta{\uparrow}^{k}\>{(k-1)}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}\ldots{0}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}}\,}_{(y\>\bar{t})\theta?}

\displaystyle s\theta

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\vphantom{()}\lambda\ldots\lambda}_{k~\text{times}}\>\underbrace{\vphantom{()}\smash{(y\>\bar{s})\theta{\uparrow}^{k}\>{(k-1)}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}\ldots{0}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}}\,}_{(y\>\bar{s})\theta?}

To show
t\theta\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s\theta
, we apply rule
4c

k
times. The rule is applicable if
(y\>\bar{t})\theta?\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}(y\>\bar{s})\theta?
. It is easy to see that this last inequality can be established
using rule
1
given that
y\>\bar{t}\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}y\>\bar{s}
, using
the induction hypothesis to compare pairs
t_{i},s_{i}
and using
Lemma
31
for the pairs
{j}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta},{j}{\uparrow}_{\!\eta}
of (identical)
De Bruijn indices introduced by
\eta
-expansion.

Cases

2a
,
2b
,
2c
,

2e
,
3a
,
3b
,

3d
,
4a
,
4b
,

4d

of
\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
:
These cases are similar to cases

1a
,
1b
,
1c
,

1e
,
2a
,
2b
,

2d
,
3a
,
3b
,

3d

of
\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
.
These cases correspond to strict inequalities. We first establish
t\theta\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}t\theta?
by applying rule
4a
repeatedly.
Then we show
t\theta?\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s\theta
in the same way as in the
corresponding case of
\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
.

Rules
2d
,
3c
of

\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
:
These cases may correspond to nonstrict comparisons—for example, if the
argument tuples are equal.
We apply rule
4c
repeatedly to
eliminate any
\lambda
s on both sides.
If there are any
\lambda
s remaining on the left, proceed as in the previous
case (
2a
,
2b
, etc.). Otherwise,
the rest of the proof is similar to case

1d
or
2c
of

\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
.

Rules
4c
of
\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
:

Analogous to case
3c
of
\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
.

Theorem 45
.

The relation
\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
coincides with
\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
on monomorphic preterms.
The relation
\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
coincides with
\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
on monomorphic preterms.

Proof 5.8
.

One direction of the equivalences follows by
Theorem
44
. It remains to show that

t\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
implies
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
and that
t\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
implies
t\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
. The proof is by induction on the definition
of
\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
and
\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
. It is easy to see that every case
in the definition of
\succ_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
corresponds a case in the definition of

\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
and every case in the definition of
\succsim_{\mathsf{m\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
corresponds
a case in the definition of
\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}
.

Lemma 46
.

If
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
, then
t\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
.

Proof 5.9
.

Analogous to Lemma
32
.

Theorem 47
.

If
t\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}u
and
u\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
, then
t\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
.
If moreover
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}u
or
u\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
, then
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
.

Proof 5.10
.

Analogous to Theorem
36
,
using the fact that
\unrhd
is transitive.

Theorem 48
.

Let
t\succ_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}s
.
Let
\theta
be a substitution such that all variables in

t\theta
and
s\theta
are nonfunctional term variables.
Let
s\theta
contain a nonfunctional variable
x

outside of parameters.
Then
t\theta
must also contain
x
outside of parameters.

Proof 5.11
.

By Theorems
37
and
44
.

6.
Examples

Let us see how
\lambda
KBO and
\lambda
LPO work on some realistic examples.
All the examples below are beyond the reach of the derived higher-order KBO and
LPO presented by Bentkamp et al.
[
3
]
and
implemented in Zipperposition.

{exa}

Consider the following clause:

\mathsf{p}\>(\lambda\>\mathsf{f}\>(y\>0))\mathrel{\lor}\mathsf{p}\>(\lambda\>y\>0)
.
We would like to orient the two literals. The orientation

\mathsf{p}\>(\lambda\>\mathsf{f}\>(y\>0))\succ\mathsf{p}\>(\lambda\>y\>0)

appears more promising.
With
\lambda
KBO, assuming a weight of
1
for
\mathsf{f}
,
\mathsf{p}
,

\lambda
, and
0
and argument coefficients of
1
, via a mechanical
application of Definition
5.1
we get the polynomial inequality

1+1+1+1+\mathbf{w}_{y}+\mathbf{k}_{y,1}(1-1)\kern 0.83328pt>\kern 0.83328pt1+1+1+\mathbf{w}_{y}+\mathbf{k}_{y,1}(1-1)
.
In other words,
1>0
.
With
\lambda
LPO, the desired orientation is easy to derive since

y\>0
is a subterm of
\mathsf{f}\>(y\>0)
.

{exa}

The following equation defines the transitivity of a relation
r

0.17224pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}\kern-5.00002pt\raise 0.08612pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}\kern-5.00002pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}\kern-5.00002pt\raise-0.08612pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}\kern-5.00002pt\raise-0.17224pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}\kern-5.00002pt\kern-0.08612pt\raise 0.17224pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}\kern-5.00002pt\raise 0.08612pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}\kern-5.00002pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}\kern-5.00002pt\raise-0.08612pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}\kern-5.00002pt\raise-0.17224pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}\kern-5.00002pt\kern-0.08612pt\raise 0.17224pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}\kern-5.00002pt\raise 0.08612pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}\kern-5.00002pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}\kern-5.00002pt\raise-0.08612pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}\kern-5.00002pt\raise-0.17224pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}\kern-5.00002pt\kern-0.08612pt\raise 0.17224pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}\kern-5.00002pt\raise 0.08612pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}\kern-5.00002pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}\kern-5.00002pt\raise-0.08612pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}\kern-5.00002pt\raise-0.17224pt\hbox{$\rightarrow$}\kern 0.17224pt}r\>2\>0)))
.
We start with
\lambda
KBO.
Assume a weight of 1 for
\mathsf{trans}
,
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
\forall
,
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
\land
,

\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
\to
,
\lambda
,
0
, … and argument coefficients of
1
.
After simplification, the polynomial inequality for a right-to-left orientation
becomes

\mathbf{w}_{r}+4\kern 0.83328pt<\kern 0.83328pt3\mathbf{w}_{r}+14
,
which clearly holds. Is there a way to orient the equation from left to right
instead? There is if we make
\mathsf{trans}
heavier and set a
higher weight coefficient on its argument. Take
\cal w(\mathsf{trans})=5
and

\cal k(\mathsf{trans},1)=3
. Then we get

3\mathbf{w}_{r}+15\kern 0.83328pt>\kern 0.83328pt3\mathbf{w}_{r}+14
.
In contrast,
\lambda
LPO cannot orient the equation from left to right; among
the proof obligations that emerge are
r\>1\>0\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}^{?}r\>2\>1
and
r\>1\>0\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}^{?}r\>2\>0
,
and these cannot be discharged.

{exa}

In the clause

y\>(\lambda\>\mathsf{a}\>0)\mathrel{\lor}\lnot\>y\>(\lambda\>\mathsf{f}\>(\mathsf{sk}(y)\>0))\mathrel{\lor}\lnot\>y\>(\lambda\>0)
,
we would like to make
y\>(\lambda\>\mathsf{a}\>0)
the maximal literal.
The apparent difficulty is the presence of the variable
y
deep inside the
second literal. Fortunately, since it occurs in a parameter, it has no impact on
the
\lambda
KBO weight. We are then free to make the symbol
\mathsf{a}
as heavy
as we want to ensure that
\lambda\>\mathsf{a}\>0
is heavier than
\lambda\>\mathsf{f}\>(\mathsf{sk}(y)\>0)
and
\lambda\>0
, both of which have
constant weights. A similar approach can be taken with
\lambda
LPO, using the
precedence instead of weights.

{exa}

In functional programming, the map function on lists is defined recursively by

\displaystyle\mathsf{map}\>(\lambda\>f\>0)\>\mathsf{nil}

\displaystyle\approx\mathsf{nil}

\displaystyle\mathsf{map}\>(\lambda\>f\>0)\>(\mathsf{cons}\>x\>\mathit{xs})

\displaystyle\approx\mathsf{cons}\>(f\>x)\>(\mathsf{map}\>(\lambda\>f\>0)\>\mathit{xs})

The first equation is easy to orient from left to right. Not so for the second
equation. With
\lambda
KBO, to compensate for the two occurrences of
f
on the
right-hand side, we would need to set a coefficient of at least
2
on

\mathsf{map}
’s first argument; this would make the left-hand side heavier but
would also make the right-hand side even heavier. In general, KBO is rather
ineffective at orienting recursive equations from left to right.
With
\lambda
LPO, the issue is the undischargeable proof obligation
f\>0\succsim_{\mathsf{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1.05]{${}_{\mathsf{y}}$}}\kern-0.5833ptlpo}}^{?}f\>x
.

With both orders, a right-to-left orientation is also problematic, because
f

might ignore its argument, resulting in an
x
on the left-hand side with no
matching
x
on the right-hand side. On the positive side, superposition provers
rarely need to orient recursive equations in their full generality. Instead, the
calculus considers instances of the equations where higher-order
variables are replaced by concrete functions. These equation instances are often
orientable from right to left.

7.
Naive Algorithms

The definitions given in Sect.
5
provide a sound
theoretical basis for an implementation, but they should not be followed
naively.
Our algorithms below perform the comparisons
t\succ s
,
t\succsim s
,

t=s
,
t\precsim s
, and
t\prec s
simultaneously, reusing
subcomputations. Typically, given terms
s,t
, a superposition prover might
need to check both
t\succsim s
and
t\precsim s
.

As our programming language, we use a functional programming notation inspired
by Standard ML, OCaml, and Haskell.
First, we need a type to represent the result of a comparison:

datatype

\mathsf{cmp}

=

\mathsf{G}

\mid

\mathsf{GE}

\mid

\mathsf{E}

\mid

\mathsf{LE}

\mid

\mathsf{L}

\mid

\mathsf{U}

The six values represent
\succ
,
\succsim
,
=
,
\precsim
,
\prec
,
and “unknown” or “incomparable,” respectively.

7.1.
\bm{\lambda}
KBO

Our first algorithm will perform
\lambda
KBO comparisons in both directions
simultaneously.
The following auxiliary functions are used to combine an imprecise comparison
result
\mathsf{GE}
or
\mathsf{LE}
with another result, using
\mathsf{U}

on mismatch:

function

\mathsf{mergeWithGE}\;\mathit{cmp}

:=

match

\mathit{cmp}

with

\mathsf{L}

\mid

\mathsf{LE}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{U}

\mid

\mathsf{E}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{GE}

\mid

\_

\Rightarrow

\mathit{cmp}

end

function

\mathsf{mergeWithLE}\;\mathit{cmp}

:=

match

\mathit{cmp}

with

\mathsf{G}

\mid

\mathsf{GE}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{U}

\mid

\mathsf{E}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{LE}

\mid

\_

\Rightarrow

\mathit{cmp}

end

The lexicographic extension of a comparison operator working with our
comparison type is defined as follows:

function

\mathsf{lexExt}\;\mathit{op}\;\bar{b}\;\bar{a}

:=

match

\bar{b}
,
\bar{a}

with

[]
,
[]

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{E}

\mid

b::\bar{b}^{\prime}
,
a::\bar{a}^{\prime}

\Rightarrow

match

\mathit{op}\;b\;a

with

\mathsf{G}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{G}

\mid

\mathsf{GE}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{mergeWithGE}\;(\mathsf{lexExt}\;\mathit{op}\;\bar{b}^{\prime}\;\bar{a}^{\prime})

\mid

\mathsf{E}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{lexExt}\;\mathit{op}\;\bar{b}^{\prime}\;\bar{a}^{\prime}

\mid

\mathsf{LE}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{mergeWithLE}\;(\mathsf{lexExt}\;\mathit{op}\;\bar{b}^{\prime}\;\bar{a}^{\prime})

\mid

\mathsf{L}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{L}

\mid

\mathsf{U}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{U}

end

end

We need to support only the case in which both lists have the same length.

Next to the lexicographic extension, we also define a form of componentwise
extension of a comparison operator:

function

\mathsf{smooth}\;\mathit{cmp}

:=

\mathit{cmp}

\mathsf{L}

\mathsf{LE}

\Rightarrow

\mathit{cmp}

function

\mathsf{cwExt}\;\mathit{op}

:=

\mathsf{lexExt}\;(\textbf{fun}\;b\;a\Rightarrow\mathsf{smooth}\;(\mathit{op}\;b\;a))

We need to support only the case in which both lists have the same length.

Next, we need a function that checks whether
\unrhd
or its inverse

\mathsf{considerPoly}\;t\;s\;\mathit{cmp}

\mathit{cmp}

\mathit{cmp}

\mathsf{U}

\mid

\Rightarrow

\mathit{cmp}

The function for checking inequalities is very simple:

function

\mathsf{surelyNonneg}\;w

:=

all coefficients in the standard form of
w
are
\geq 0

It returns
\mathsf{true}
if all the polynomials in the list are certainly
nonnegative for any values of the indeterminates and
\mathsf{false}
if this is
not known to be the case, either because there exists a counterexample or
because the approach is too imprecise to tell.

Polynomials in standard form have at most one
constant monomial
: a
monomial consisting of only a coefficient with no indeterminates. If absent, it
is taken to be 0. The weight comparison can be refined by considering the sign
of the constant monomial in the difference
\cal W(t)-\cal W(s)
. If the sign
is positive,
\cal W(t)\geq\cal W(s)
actually means
\cal W(t)>\cal W(s)
.
If the sign is negative,
\cal W(t)\leq\cal W(s)
actually means
W(t)<\cal W(s)
.

function

\mathsf{analyzeWeightDiff}\;w

:=

match

\mathsf{surelyNonneg}\;w
,
\mathsf{surelyNonneg}\;(-w)

with

\mathsf{false}
,
\mathsf{false}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{U}

\mid

\mathsf{true}
,
\mathsf{false}

\Rightarrow

if
the constant monomial of
w
is
>0

then

\mathsf{G}

else

\mathsf{GE}

\mid

\mathsf{false}
,
\mathsf{true}

\Rightarrow

if
the constant monomial of
w
is
<0

then

\mathsf{L}

else

\mathsf{LE}

\mid

\mathsf{true}
,
\mathsf{true}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{E}

end

8.
Optimized Algorithms

Another improvement, embodied by a separate pair of algorithms, consists of
following Löchner’s refinement approach

[
8
,
9
]
. For the standard KBO and LPO, his
comparison algorithms are respectively linear and quadratic in the size of the
input terms. The use of polynomials instead of integers in the
\lambda
KBO
makes the computation slightly more expensive, but we can nonetheless benefit
from tupling.

8.1.
\bm{\lambda}
KBO

The naive bidirectional algorithm for
\lambda
KBO is wasteful because it
recursively recomputes preterm weights. If
t=\mathsf{f}(\bar{t})
, the subterm

t_{i}
’s weight is computed first in the main function by the call to

\mathsf{analyzeWeightDiff}
and then possibly again in

\mathsf{compareShapes}
, when
\mathsf{compareTerms}
is called to break ties.
Although a factor of 2 might not sound particularly expensive, the factor is
higher for the subterms’ subterms, their subsubterms, and so on. Thus, the
native algorithm is quadratic in the size of the input
preterms
[
8
]
.

Our solution, inspired by Löchner
[
8
]
, consists of
interleaving the two passes: computing the weights and comparing the shapes.
The information for the passes is stored in a tuple. In this way, the subterms’
weights can be shared between the passes. At the end of the combined pass, we
can look at the tuple and determine what result to return.

First, we need to extend the lexicographic and componentwise extension functions
to thread through additional information—in our case, weights—returned by
the operator
\mathit{op}
as the first component of a pair, the second
component being the comparison result.

function

\mathsf{lexExtData}\;\mathit{op}\;\bar{b}\;\bar{a}

:=

match

\bar{b}
,
\bar{a}

with

[]
,
[]

\Rightarrow

([],\mathsf{E})

\mid

b::\bar{b}^{\prime}
,
a::\bar{a}^{\prime}

\Rightarrow

match

\mathit{op}\;b\;a

with

(w{,}\;\mathsf{G})

\Rightarrow

([w],\mathsf{G})

\mid

(w{,}\;\mathsf{GE})

\Rightarrow

let

(\bar{w}{,}\;\mathit{cmp}):=\mathsf{lexExtData}\;\mathit{op}\;\bar{b}^{\prime}\;\bar{a}^{\prime}

in

(w::\bar{w}{,}\;\mathsf{mergeWithGE}\;\mathit{cmp})

\mid

(w{,}\;\mathsf{E})

\Rightarrow

let

(\bar{w}{,}\;\mathit{cmp}):=\mathsf{lexExtData}\;\mathit{op}\;\bar{b}^{\prime}\;\bar{a}^{\prime}

in

(w::\bar{w}{,}\;\mathit{cmp})

\mid

(w{,}\;\mathsf{LE})

\Rightarrow

let

(\bar{w}{,}\;\mathit{cmp}):=\mathsf{lexExtData}\;\mathit{op}\;\bar{b}^{\prime}\;\bar{a}^{\prime}

in

(w::\bar{w}{,}\;\mathsf{mergeWithLE}\;\mathit{cmp})

\mid

(w{,}\;\mathsf{L})

\Rightarrow

([w]{,}\;\mathsf{L})

\mid

(w{,}\;\mathsf{U})

\Rightarrow

([w]{,}\;\mathsf{U})

end

end

function

\mathsf{cwExtData}\;\mathit{op}

:=

\mathsf{lexExtData}\;(\textbf{fun}\;b\;a\Rightarrow{}

let

(w{,}\;\mathit{cmp})=\mathit{op}\;b\;a

in

(w{,}\;\mathsf{smooth}\;\mathit{cmp}))

In the above, the operator
::
(“cons”) prepends an element to a list.

The auxiliary function
\mathsf{considerWeight}
resembles the unoptimized

\mathsf{compareTerms}
, but it uses its arguments
w
and
\mathit{cmp}

instead of recomputing them, where
\mathit{cmp}
is the result of a shape
comparison.

function

\mathsf{considerWeight}\;w\;\mathit{cmp}

:=

(w{,}

match

\mathsf{analyzeWeightDiff}\;w

with

(w{,}

\mathsf{G}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{G}

(w{,}

\mid

\mathsf{GE}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{mergeWithGE}\;\mathit{cmp}

(w{,}

\mid

\mathsf{E}

\Rightarrow

\mathit{cmp}

(w{,}

\mid

\mathsf{LE}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{mergeWithLE}\;\mathit{cmp}

(w{,}

\mid

\mathsf{L}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{L}

(w{,}

\mid

\mathsf{U}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{U}

(w{,}

end
)

The core of the code consists of two mutually recursive functions:
\mathsf{processArgs}
and
\mathsf{processTerms}
. They compute weights and
compare shapes, returning pairs of the form
(w{,}\;\mathit{cmp})
, where

\mathit{cmp}
takes both the preterms’ weights and their shapes into account. The
code for
\mathsf{processTerms}
follows the structure of the unoptimized

\mathsf{compareShape}
but is instrumented to also compute weights. It calls

\mathsf{processArgs}
to compare argument lists. In
\mathsf{processArgs}
, the
weights computed as part of the lexicographic comparison are reused and extended
with any missing weights if the comparison ended before the end of the lists
(i.e., if
m<n
).

function

\mathsf{processArgs}\;\bar{t}_{n}\;\bar{s}_{n}

:=

let

(\bar{w}_{m}{,}\;\mathit{cmp}):=\mathsf{lexExtData}\;\mathsf{processTerms}\;\bar{t}_{n}\;\bar{s}_{n}

in

\mathsf{considerWeight}\;(\sum_{i=1}^{m}w_{i}+\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}(\cal W(t_{i})-\cal W(s_{i})))\;\mathit{cmp}

function

\mathsf{processTerms}\;t\;s

:=

match

t
,
s

with

y\>\bar{t}
,
x\>\bar{s}

\Rightarrow

if

y=x

then

if

\bar{t}
are steady
then

let

(\bar{w}{,}\;\mathit{cmp}):=\mathsf{cwExtData}\;\mathsf{processTerms}\;\bar{t}\;\bar{s}

in

\mathsf{considerWeight}\;\bigl(\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{|\bar{t}|}\mathbf{k}_{y,i}\,w_{i}\bigr)\;\mathit{cmp}

else

\mathsf{considerWeight}\;(\cal W(t)-\cal W(s))\;\mathsf{U}

else

\mathsf{considerWeight}\;(\cal W(t)-\cal W(s))\;\mathsf{U}

\mid

y\>\_
,
\_

\mid

\_
,
x\>\_

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{considerWeight}\;(\cal W(t)-\cal W(s))\;\mathsf{U}

\mid

\lambda\langle\upsilon\rangle\>t^{\prime}
,
\lambda\langle\tau\rangle\>s^{\prime}

\Rightarrow

match

\mathsf{compareTypes}\;\upsilon\;\tau

with

\mathsf{E}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{processTerms}\;t^{\prime}\;s^{\prime}

\mid

\mathit{cmp}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{considerWeight}\;(\cal W(t^{\prime})-\cal W(s^{\prime}))\;\mathit{cmp}

end

\mid

\lambda\>\_
,
\_

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{considerWeight}\;(\cal W(t)-\cal W(s))\;(\mathsf{considerPoly}\;t\;s\;\mathsf{G})

\mid

n\>\_
,
\lambda\>\_

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{considerWeight}\;(\cal W(t)-\cal W(s))\;(\mathsf{considerPoly}\;t\;s\;\mathsf{L})

\mid

n\>\bar{t}
,

\Rightarrow

if

n>m

then

\mathsf{considerWeight}\;(\cal W(t)-\cal W(s))\;(\mathsf{considerPoly}\;t\;s\;\mathsf{G})

else if

n<m

then

\mathsf{considerWeight}\;(\cal W(t)-\cal W(s))\;(\mathsf{considerPoly}\;t\;s\;\mathsf{L})

else

\mathsf{processArgs}\;\bar{t}\;\bar{s}

\mid

n\>\_
,
\mathsf{f}\langle\_\rangle(\_)\>\_

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{considerWeight}\;(\cal W(t)-\cal W(s))\;(\mathsf{considerPoly}\;t\;s\;\mathsf{G})

\mid

\mathsf{g}\langle\bar{\upsilon}\rangle(\bar{w})\>\bar{t}
,
\mathsf{f}\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle(\bar{u})\>\bar{s}

\Rightarrow

match

\mathsf{compareSyms}\;\mathsf{g}\;\mathsf{f}

with

\mathsf{E}

\Rightarrow

match

\mathsf{lexExt}\;\mathsf{compareTypes}\;\bar{\upsilon}\;\bar{\tau}

with

\mathsf{E}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{processArgs}\;(\bar{w}\cdot\bar{t})\;(\bar{u}\cdot\bar{s})

\mid

\mathit{cmp}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{considerWeight}\;(\cal W(t)-\cal W(s))\;(\mathsf{considerPoly}\;t\;s\;\mathit{cmp})

end

\mid

\mathit{cmp}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{considerWeight}\;(\cal W(t)-\cal W(s))\;(\mathsf{considerPoly}\;t\;s\;\mathit{cmp})

end

\mid

\mathsf{g}\langle\_\rangle(\_)\>\_
,
\_

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{considerWeight}\;(\cal W(t)-\cal W(s))\;(\mathsf{considerPoly}\;t\;s\;\mathsf{L})

end

When calling
\mathsf{processTerms}
to compare two preterms, we would normally
ignore the
w
component of the result and only consider
\mathit{cmp}
, which
should be equal to what the untupled
\mathsf{compareTerms}
would return.

One last point to discuss is the representation of polynomials. In the standard
KBO, multisets of variables must be compared. These can be seen as polynomials
of degree 1. Löchner’s approach for the KBO variable check is to use an array
indexed by a finite variable set
X
. Clearly, this technique does not scale to
polynomials of arbitrarily high degrees. Instead of arrays, we can use maps or
hash tables indexed by sorted lists of indeterminates. With a reasonable map
implementation, this would replace an
\mathrm{O}(n)
complexity with

\mathrm{O}(n\log n)
, where
n=\left|s\right|+\left|t\right|
, the size of
the input preterms.

One of Löchner’s ideas that also applies in our setting is to maintain two
counters indicating how many monomials are nonnegative or nonpositive in the
current polynomial expressed in standard form. These counters must be updated
whenever the map or hash table is modified. The two calls to

\mathsf{surelyNonneg}
in
\mathsf{analyzeWeightDiff}
can then be replaced by
two conditions that each check whether a counter is 0.

8.2.
\bm{\lambda}
LPO

The naive bidirectional algorithm has exponential complexity because of the
overlapping computations of
\mathsf{checkArgs}
and
\mathsf{checkSubs}
. Our
solution, again inspired by Löchner
[
9
]
, consists of
postponing the checks and avoiding redundant comparisons. Specifically, our
algorithm below draws inspiration from Löchner’s
\mathsf{clpo}_{6}
.

We start with a simple auxiliary function:

function

\mathsf{flip}\;\mathit{cmp}

:=

match

\mathit{cmp}

with

G

\Rightarrow

L

\mid

GE

\Rightarrow

LE

\mid

E

\Rightarrow

E

\mid

LE

\Rightarrow

GE

\mid

L

\Rightarrow

G

\mid

U

\Rightarrow

U

end

The following six functions are mutually recursive. The main function is
called
\mathsf{compareTerms}
, as in Section
7
.

function

\mathsf{checkSubs}\;\bar{t}\;s

:=

\exists i.\;\mathsf{compareTerms}\;t_{i}\;s\in\{\mathsf{G},\mathsf{GE},\mathsf{E}\}

function

\mathsf{compareSubsBothWays}\;t\;\bar{t}\;s\;\bar{s}

:=

if

\mathsf{checkSubs}\;\bar{t}\;s

then

\mathsf{G}

else if

\mathsf{checkSubs}\;\bar{s}\;t

then

\mathsf{L}

else

\mathsf{U}

function

\mathsf{compareRest}\;t\;\bar{s}

:=

match

\bar{s}

with

[]

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{G}

\mid

s::\bar{s}^{\prime}

\Rightarrow

match

\mathsf{compareTerms}\;t\;s

with

\mathsf{G}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{compareRest}\;t\;\bar{s}^{\prime}

\mid

\mathsf{E}

\mid

\mathsf{LE}

\mid

\mathsf{L}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{L}

\mid

\mathsf{GE}

\mid

\mathsf{U}

\Rightarrow

if

\mathsf{checkSubs}\;\bar{s}^{\prime}\;t

then

\mathsf{L}

else

\mathsf{U}

end

end

function

\mathsf{compareRegularArgs}\;t\;\bar{t}\;s\;\bar{s}

:=

match

\bar{t}
,
\bar{s}

with

[]
,
[]

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{E}

\mid

t_{1}::\bar{t}^{\prime}
,
s_{1}::\bar{s}^{\prime}

\Rightarrow

match

\mathsf{compareTerms}\;t_{1}\;s_{1}

with

\mathsf{G}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{compareRest}\;t\;\bar{s}^{\prime}

\mid

\mathsf{GE}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{mergeWithGE}\;(\mathsf{compareRegularArgs}\;t\;\bar{t}^{\prime}\;s\;\bar{s}^{\prime})

\mid

\mathsf{E}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{compareRegularArgs}\;t\;\bar{t}^{\prime}\;s\;\bar{s}^{\prime}

\mid

\mathsf{LE}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{mergeWithLE}\;(\mathsf{compareRegularArgs}\;t\;\bar{t}^{\prime}\;s\;\bar{s}^{\prime})

\mid

\mathsf{L}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{flip}\;(\mathsf{compareRest}\;s\;\bar{t}^{\prime})

\mid

\mathsf{U}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{compareSubsBothWays}\;t\;\bar{t}^{\prime}\;s\;\bar{s}^{\prime}

end

end

function

\mathsf{compareArgs}\;t\;\bar{v}\;\bar{t}\;s\;\bar{u}\;\bar{s}

:=

match

\bar{v}
,
\bar{u}

with

[]
,
[]

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{compareRegularArgs}\;t\;\bar{t}\;s\;\bar{s}

\mid

v_{1}::\bar{v}^{\prime}
,
u_{1}::\bar{u}^{\prime}

\Rightarrow

match

\mathsf{compareTerms}\;v_{1}\;u_{1}

with

\mathsf{G}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{compareRest}\;t\;\bar{s}

\mid

\mathsf{GE}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{mergeWithGE}\;(\mathsf{compareArgs}\;t\;\bar{v}^{\prime}\;\bar{t}\;s\;\bar{u}^{\prime}\;\bar{s})

\mid

\mathsf{E}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{compareArgs}\;t\;\bar{v}^{\prime}\;\bar{t}\;s\;\bar{u}^{\prime}\;\bar{s}

\mid

\mathsf{LE}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{mergeWithLE}\;(\mathsf{compareArgs}\;t\;\bar{v}^{\prime}\;\bar{t}\;s\;\bar{u}^{\prime}\;\bar{s})

\mid

\mathsf{L}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{flip}\;(\mathsf{compareRest}\;s\;\bar{t})

\mid

\mathsf{U}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{compareSubsBothWays}\;t\;\bar{t}\;s\;\bar{s}

end

end

function

\mathsf{compareTerms}\;t\;s

:=

match

t
,
s

with

y\>\bar{t}
,
x\>\bar{s}

\Rightarrow

if

y=x\mathrel{\land}\bar{t}
are steady
then

\mathsf{cwExt}\;\mathsf{compareTerms}\;\bar{t}\;\bar{s}

else

\mathsf{U}

\mid

y\>\_
,
\mathsf{f}\langle\_\rangle(\_)\>\bar{s}

\mid

y\>\_
,
m\>\bar{s}

\Rightarrow

if

\mathsf{checkSubs}\;\bar{s}\;t

then

\mathsf{L}

else

\mathsf{U}

\mid

y\>\_
,
\lambda\>s^{\prime}

\Rightarrow

if

\mathsf{checkSubs}\;[s^{\prime}]\;t

then

\mathsf{L}

else

\mathsf{U}

\mid

\mathsf{g}\langle\_\rangle(\_)\>\bar{t}
,
x\>\_

\Rightarrow

if

\mathsf{checkSubs}\;\bar{t}\;s

then

\mathsf{G}

else

\mathsf{U}

\mid

\mathsf{g}\langle\bar{\upsilon}\rangle(\bar{w})\>\bar{t}
,
\mathsf{f}\langle\bar{\tau}\rangle(\bar{u})\>\bar{s}

\Rightarrow

match

\mathsf{compareSyms}\;\mathsf{g}\;\mathsf{f}

with

\mathsf{G}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{considerPolyBelowWS}\;\mathsf{g}\;t\;s\;(\mathsf{compareRest}\;t\;\bar{s})

\mid

\mathsf{E}

\Rightarrow

match

\mathsf{lexExt}\;\mathsf{compareTypes}\;\bar{\upsilon}\;\bar{\tau}

with

\mathsf{G}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{considerPolyBelowWS}\;\mathsf{g}\;t\;s\;(\mathsf{compareRest}\;t\;\bar{s})

\mid

\mathsf{E}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{compareArgs}\;t\;\bar{w}\;\bar{t}\;s\;\bar{u}\;\bar{s}

\mid

\mathsf{L}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{considerPolyBelowWS}\;\mathsf{g}\;t\;s\;(\mathsf{flip}\;(\mathsf{compareRest}\;s\;\bar{t}))

\mid

\mathsf{U}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{compareSubsBothWays}\;t\;\bar{t}\;s\;\bar{s}

end

\mid

\mathsf{L}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{considerPolyBelowWS}\;\mathsf{g}\;t\;s\;(\mathsf{flip}\;(\mathsf{compareRest}\;t\;\bar{s}))

\mid

\mathsf{U}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{compareSubsBothWays}\;t\;\bar{t}\;s\;\bar{s}

end

\mid

\mathsf{g}\langle\_\rangle(\_)\>\bar{t}
,
m\>\bar{s}

\Rightarrow

if

\mathsf{f}>\mathsf{ws}

then

\mathsf{compareRest}\;t\;\bar{s}

else

\mathsf{considerPoly}\;t\;s\;(\mathsf{flip}\;(\mathsf{compareRest}\;s\;\bar{t}))

\mid

\mathsf{g}\langle\_\rangle(\_)\>\bar{t}
,
\lambda\>s^{\prime}

\Rightarrow

if

\mathsf{f}>\mathsf{ws}

then

\mathsf{compareRest}\;t\;[s^{\prime}]

else

\mathsf{considerPoly}\;t\;s\;(\mathsf{flip}\;(\mathsf{compareRest}\;s\;\bar{t}))

\mid

n\>\bar{t}
,
x\>\_

\Rightarrow

if

\mathsf{checkSubs}\;\bar{t}\;s

then

\mathsf{G}

else

\mathsf{U}

\mid

n\>\bar{t}
,
\mathsf{f}\langle\_\rangle(\_)\>\bar{s}

\Rightarrow

if

\mathsf{f}>\mathsf{ws}

then

\mathsf{flip}\;(\mathsf{compareRest}\;s\;\bar{t})

else

\mathsf{considerPoly}\;t\;s\;(\mathsf{compareRest}\;t\;\bar{s})

\mid

n\>\bar{t}
,
m\>\bar{s}

\Rightarrow

if

n>m

then

\mathsf{considerPoly}\;t\;s\;(\mathsf{compareRest}\;t\;\bar{s})

else if

n=m

then

\mathsf{compareRegularArgs}\;t\;\bar{t}\;s\;\bar{s}

else

\mathsf{considerPoly}\;t\;s\;(\mathsf{flip}\;(\mathsf{compareRest}\;s\;\bar{t}))

\mid

n\>\_
,
\lambda\>s^{\prime}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{compareRest}\;t\;[s^{\prime}]

\mid

\lambda\>t^{\prime}
,
x\>\_

\Rightarrow

if

\mathsf{checkSubs}\;[t^{\prime}]\;s

then

\mathsf{G}

else

\mathsf{U}

\mid

\lambda\>t^{\prime}
,
\mathsf{f}\langle\_\rangle(\_)\>\bar{s}

\Rightarrow

if

\mathsf{f}>\mathsf{ws}

then

\mathsf{flip}\;(\mathsf{compareRest}\;s\;[t^{\prime}])

else

\mathsf{considerPoly}\;t\;s\;(\mathsf{compareRest}\;t\;\bar{s})

\mid

\lambda\>t^{\prime}
,
m\>\_

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{flip}\;(\mathsf{compareRest}\;s\;t^{\prime})

\mid

\lambda\langle\upsilon\rangle\>t^{\prime}
,
\lambda\langle\tau\rangle\>s^{\prime}

\Rightarrow

match

\mathsf{compareTypes}\;\upsilon\;\tau

with

\mathsf{G}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{compareRest}\;t\;[s^{\prime}]

\mid

\mathsf{E}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{compareTerms}\;t^{\prime}\;s^{\prime}

\mid

\mathsf{L}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{flip}\;(\mathsf{compareRest}\;s\;[t^{\prime}])

\mid

\mathsf{U}

\Rightarrow

\mathsf{compareSubsBothWays}\;t\;[t^{\prime}]\;s\;[s^{\prime}]

end

end

9.
Conclusion

We defined two new term orders,
\lambda
KBO and
\lambda
LPO, for use with

\lambda
-superposition. We expect these new order to improve Zipperposition’s
performance, measured as both proving time and success rate.
Some of the ideas might also apply to
\lambda
-free superposition

[
2
]
and combinatory superposition

[
4
]
: Despite working on logics devoid of

\lambda
-abstractions, these proof calculi contain axiom (
Ext
) and
could benefit from the implicit
\eta
-expansion that makes its positive literal
maximal.
